Macbeth

Author: William Shakespeare
Publication Year: 1606
Genre: Tragedy

Background

"Macbeth" is one of William Shakespeare’s most famous and enduring tragedies, written around 1606 during the reign of King James I. The play explores themes of ambition, power, guilt, and the supernatural, set against the backdrop of medieval Scotland. It is often referred to as "The Scottish Play" due to theatrical superstitions surrounding its name.

The play reflects the political and social concerns of Shakespeare’s time, particularly the divine right of kings and the dangers of regicide. It also incorporates elements of the supernatural, which would have resonated with King James I’s fascination with witchcraft and his authorship of Daemonologie.

Structure and Style

Form: Five-act structure, typical of Shakespearean drama.

Language: Written in blank verse (unrhymed iambic pentameter) with occasional rhymed couplets and prose.

Tone: Dark, foreboding, and tragic.

Genre: Tragedy, with elements of the supernatural and psychological drama.

Plot Summary

Act I: The Prophecy and the Seed of Ambition

The play opens with three witches on a desolate heath, setting the tone of foreboding and supernatural influence. They chant:

"Fair is foul, and foul is fair."

This paradox introduces the theme of moral ambiguity and the inversion of natural order.

The witches meet Macbeth and Banquo, two Scottish generals returning from a victorious battle. They prophesy that Macbeth will become the Thane of Cawdor and eventually the King of Scotland, while Banquo’s descendants will inherit the throne. Shortly after, Macbeth is indeed granted the title of Thane of Cawdor, sparking his ambition.

Lady Macbeth, upon hearing of the prophecy, becomes the driving force behind Macbeth’s ambition. She persuades him to murder King Duncan, despite his initial hesitation:

"If it were done when ’tis done, then ’twere well it were done quickly."

This soliloquy reveals Macbeth’s internal conflict, torn between his ambition and his moral conscience.

Act II: The Murder of Duncan

In Act II, Macbeth succumbs to his ambition and murders King Duncan in his sleep. The act is shrouded in darkness, both literal and metaphorical, symbolizing the moral corruption of Macbeth’s soul.

After the murder, Macbeth is immediately consumed by guilt and paranoia:

"Will all great Neptune’s ocean wash this blood clean from my hand?"

This vivid imagery reflects his psychological torment, as he realizes the irreversible nature of his crime.

Lady Macbeth, in contrast, appears composed and practical, dismissing his guilt:

"A little water clears us of this deed."

However, this calm façade will later crumble as guilt consumes her as well.

Act III: The Rise of Tyranny

Having seized the throne, Macbeth becomes increasingly paranoid about the witches’ prophecy that Banquo’s descendants will inherit the throne. He arranges for Banquo and his son, Fleance, to be murdered.

While Banquo is killed, Fleance escapes, leaving Macbeth’s fears unresolved. At a banquet, Macbeth is haunted by Banquo’s ghost, which only he can see. His erratic behavior alarms his guests and marks the beginning of his descent into madness:

"Thou canst not say I did it: never shake thy gory locks at me!"

This scene underscores Macbeth’s growing instability and the psychological consequences of his actions.

Act IV: The Witches’ Deception

Macbeth seeks out the witches again, desperate for reassurance about his future. They provide him with three cryptic apparitions:

An armed head warns him to beware of Macduff.

A bloody child tells him that no man born of a woman will harm him.

A crowned child holding a tree assures him that he will not be defeated until Birnam Wood comes to Dunsinane Hill.

These prophecies give Macbeth a false sense of invincibility, as he interprets them literally. However, the witches also show him a vision of Banquo’s descendants as future kings, deepening his paranoia.

Meanwhile, Macbeth orders the brutal murder of Macduff’s family, an act of senseless cruelty that solidifies his transformation into a tyrant.

Act V: The Downfall of Macbeth

In the final act, Lady Macbeth is consumed by guilt and descends into madness. She sleepwalks and obsessively tries to wash imaginary blood from her hands:

"Out, damned spot! Out, I say!"

This iconic line reveals the psychological toll of her complicity in Duncan’s murder. Her death, likely by suicide, marks the tragic culmination of her character arc.

Macbeth, now isolated and defiant, prepares for battle against the advancing forces of Malcolm (Duncan’s son) and Macduff. He clings to the witches’ prophecies, believing himself invincible. However, the prophecies unravel:

Malcolm’s army disguises itself with branches from Birnam Wood, making it appear as though the forest is moving toward Dunsinane.

Macduff reveals that he was born via Caesarean section, meaning he is not "born of a woman" in the conventional sense.

In the final confrontation, Macduff kills Macbeth, fulfilling the prophecy and restoring order to Scotland. Malcolm is crowned king, symbolizing the triumph of justice and the restoration of natural order.

Themes

1\. Ambition and Power

The central theme of Macbeth is the corrupting influence of unchecked ambition. Macbeth’s desire for power drives him to commit heinous acts, leading to his eventual downfall. Lady Macbeth’s ambition is equally destructive, as it pushes her to manipulate her husband and disregard moral boundaries.

2\. Guilt and Conscience

Both Macbeth and Lady Macbeth are haunted by guilt, which manifests in hallucinations, paranoia, and madness. Macbeth’s vision of the bloody dagger and Banquo’s ghost, as well as Lady Macbeth’s sleepwalking, symbolize the psychological consequences of their crimes.

3\. The Supernatural

The witches play a pivotal role in the play, representing the forces of fate and the unknown. Their cryptic prophecies manipulate Macbeth, but they also highlight his free will, as he chooses to act on their predictions.

4\. The Inversion of Natural Order

The murder of Duncan disrupts the natural order, leading to chaos in Scotland. This theme is reinforced by imagery of darkness, unnatural events (e.g., horses eating each other), and the moral decay of the characters.

5\. Masculinity and Gender Roles

Lady Macbeth challenges traditional gender roles, questioning her husband’s masculinity to manipulate him:

"When you durst do it, then you were a man."

However, her eventual breakdown suggests the limits of her defiance against societal norms.

Character Analysis

Macbeth

Macbeth is a complex character whose tragic flaw is his unchecked ambition. Initially a loyal and honorable soldier, he becomes a tyrant consumed by guilt and paranoia. His soliloquies provide insight into his internal conflict, making him one of Shakespeare’s most psychologically rich characters.

Lady Macbeth

Lady Macbeth is a pivotal figure in the play, embodying ambition and manipulation. Her transformation from a ruthless instigator to a guilt-ridden, broken woman highlights the psychological toll of their crimes.

The Witches

The witches symbolize the supernatural and the ambiguity of fate. Their prophecies manipulate Macbeth, but they never directly instruct him to act, leaving room for interpretation about free will versus destiny.

Banquo

Banquo serves as a foil to Macbeth, representing integrity and moral restraint. His ghost symbolizes Macbeth’s guilt and the consequences of his betrayal.

Macduff

Macduff is the embodiment of justice and retribution. His role as Macbeth’s nemesis underscores the theme of restoring natural order.

Historical Context

"Macbeth" was written during the reign of King James I, who was deeply interested in witchcraft and the divine right of kings. The play reflects these concerns, with its focus on regicide, the supernatural, and the consequences of disrupting the natural order.

The character of Banquo is also significant, as James I claimed descent from Banquo’s lineage. Shakespeare’s portrayal of Banquo as noble and virtuous may have been intended to flatter the king.

Who Does What in Macbeth?

A Quick and Fun Guide to the Characters and Their Actions

Macbeth

The Ambitious Tragic Hero

Who is he?
A brave Scottish general and the Thane of Glamis, later promoted to Thane of Cawdor and crowned King of Scotland.

What does he do?

Starts as a loyal soldier but becomes consumed by ambition after hearing the witches’ prophecy.

Murders King Duncan to seize the throne.

Orders the murder of Banquo and his son, Fleance, to secure his power.

Descends into paranoia and tyranny, killing Macduff’s family.

Faces Macduff in the final battle and is killed.

Key Quote:
"Is this a dagger which I see before me, the handle toward my hand?"

Lady Macbeth

The Ambitious Manipulator

Who is she?
Macbeth’s wife, who is even more ambitious and ruthless than her husband (at least at first).

What does she do?

Pushes Macbeth to murder King Duncan by questioning his masculinity.

Helps cover up the murder by smearing blood on the guards.

Gradually succumbs to guilt and madness, obsessively trying to "wash" imaginary blood from her hands.

Dies offstage, likely by suicide.

Key Quote:
"Out, damned spot! Out, I say!"

The Three Witches

The Mysterious Prophets

Who are they?
Supernatural beings who set the events of the play in motion with their cryptic prophecies.

What do they do?

Predict that Macbeth will become King of Scotland and that Banquo’s descendants will inherit the throne.

Provide Macbeth with misleading prophecies that give him false confidence.

Represent fate, temptation, and the supernatural.

Key Quote:
"Fair is foul, and foul is fair."

King Duncan

The Good King

Who is he?
The King of Scotland at the start of the play, known for his kindness and trust.

What does he do?

Rewards Macbeth for his bravery in battle by naming him Thane of Cawdor.

Visits Macbeth’s castle, where he is murdered in his sleep.

Key Quote:
"There’s no art to find the mind’s construction in the face."

Banquo

The Noble Friend

Who is he?
A Scottish general and Macbeth’s close friend.

What does he do?

Hears the witches’ prophecy but remains skeptical and cautious.

Suspects Macbeth of foul play after Duncan’s murder.

Is murdered by Macbeth’s hired assassins, but his son, Fleance, escapes.

Returns as a ghost to haunt Macbeth at the banquet.

Key Quote:
"Thou hast it now: King, Cawdor, Glamis, all, as the weird women promised; and I fear thou play’dst most foully for ’t."

Macduff

The Avenger

Who is he?
A Scottish nobleman loyal to King Duncan and later to Malcolm.

What does he do?

Discovers Duncan’s murder and becomes suspicious of Macbeth.

Flees to England to join Malcolm and raise an army against Macbeth.

Suffers personal tragedy when Macbeth orders the murder of his wife and children.

Kills Macbeth in the final battle, fulfilling the witches’ prophecy.

Key Quote:
"Turn, hell-hound, turn!"

Malcolm

The Rightful Heir

Who is he?
King Duncan’s eldest son and the rightful heir to the throne.

What does he do?

Flees to England after Duncan’s murder, fearing for his life.

Gathers an army with Macduff’s help to overthrow Macbeth.

Becomes King of Scotland at the end of the play, restoring order.

Key Quote:
"Let grief convert to anger; blunt not the heart, enrage it."

Fleance

The Survivor

Who is he?
Banquo’s son, who is prophesied to inherit the throne.

What does he do?

Escapes when Macbeth’s assassins kill Banquo.

His survival ensures that the witches’ prophecy about Banquo’s descendants remains possible.

Key Quote:
(No direct quote, but his escape is crucial to the plot.)

The Porter

The Comic Relief

Who is he?
A servant at Macbeth’s castle who provides comic relief after Duncan’s murder.

What does he do?

Delivers a drunken monologue about the gates of hell, unwittingly commenting on the moral corruption in the castle.

Key Quote:
"Here’s a knocking indeed! If a man were porter of hell-gate, he should have old turning the key."

Lady Macduff

The Innocent Victim

Who is she?
Macduff’s wife, who is murdered along with her children on Macbeth’s orders.

What does she do?

Represents the innocent lives destroyed by Macbeth’s tyranny.

Key Quote:
"Whither should I fly? I have done no harm."

Hecate

The Queen of the Witches

Who is she?
The leader of the witches, who appears briefly to scold them for meddling with Macbeth without her involvement.

What does she do?

Adds to the supernatural atmosphere of the play.

Key Quote:
"And you all know, security is mortals’ chiefest enemy."

Siward

The Loyal Ally

Who is he?
An English general who helps Malcolm and Macduff overthrow Macbeth.

What does he do?

Leads the English forces in the final battle.

Mourns the death of his son, Young Siward, who dies fighting Macbeth.

Key Quote:
"Had I as many sons as I have hairs, I would not wish them to a fairer death."

Young Siward

The Brave Fighter

Who is he?
Siward’s son, who fights Macbeth in the final battle.

What does he do?

Dies honorably in combat against Macbeth.

Key Quote:
"The devil himself could not pronounce a title more hateful to mine ear."

Donalbain

The Forgotten Brother

Who is he?
Duncan’s younger son and Malcolm’s brother.

What does he do?

Flees to Ireland after Duncan’s murder, fearing for his life.

Does not play a significant role in the rest of the play.

Key Quote:
"There’s daggers in men’s smiles."

Act I

Who are the three voices heard at the beginning of the play?

A) The three witches

B) Macbeth, Banquo, and a soldier

C) King Duncan, Malcolm, and Donalbain

D) Macbeth, Lady Macbeth, and a messenger

Answer: A

Explanation: The three voices heard at the beginning are identified as the three witches or deformed shapes.

Where does the action in the first scene take place?

A) In a castle

B) On a battlefield

C) In a forest

D) On a heath

Answer: D

Explanation: The first scene takes place "upon the heath," which is an open, uncultivated area of land.

What is the weather condition described in the first scene?

A) A calm, sunny day

B) A terrifying storm with lightning and thunder

C) A light drizzle

D) A snowstorm

Answer: B

Explanation: The first scene describes a terrifying storm with lightning, thunder, and blinding flashes of light.

What did Macbeth do to Macdonwald, according to the sergeant's report?

A) He captured him alive

B) He beheaded him

C) He unseamed him from the navel to the jaw

D) He forced him to surrender

Answer: C

Explanation: The sergeant reports that Macbeth "unseamed him from the navel to the jaw" when describing Macbeth's victory over Macdonwald.

What did Scotland demand from Norway as a result of their victory?

A) Tribute in the form of land

B) The release of prisoners

C) A payment of ten thousand dollars

D) A peace treaty

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross states that Norway had to pay Scotland "ten thousand dollars" as a result of their defeat.

What title does King Duncan plan to give to Macbeth?

A) King of Scotland

B) Thane of Cawdor

C) Thane of Fife

D) Thane of Ross

Answer: B

Explanation: King Duncan decides to bestow the title of Thane of Cawdor upon Macbeth as a reward for his bravery.

What was the first title the witches addressed Macbeth with?

A) Thane of Glamis

B) Thane of Cawdor

C) King of Scotland

D) None of the above

Answer: A

Explanation: The first witch greets Macbeth by saying, "All hail, Macbeth! Hail to thee, Thane of Glamis!"

What did the witches prophesy about Macbeth's future?

A) He would become King of Scotland

B) He would become a powerful general

C) He would live a long and prosperous life

D) He would have many sons

Answer: A

Explanation: The third witch's greeting states, "All hail Macbeth! That shalt be king hereafter!"

What did the witches say about Banquo's future?

A) He would become king

B) He would have sons who would become kings

C) He would live a happier life than Macbeth

D) He would be greater than Macbeth

Answer: B

Explanation: The third witch tells Banquo, "Thou shalt get kings, though thou be none," suggesting his sons would become kings.

Who arrived to meet Macbeth and Banquo after their encounter with the witches?

A) King Duncan

B) Ross and Angus

C) Lady Macbeth

D) Macduff and Lennox

Answer: B

Explanation: The text mentions that "two shapes - men on horseback - emerged from the darkness" and were revealed to be Ross and Angus.

What did the first witch claim she had with her?

A) A frog's tongue

B) A pilot's thumb

C) A bat's wing

D) A wolf's tooth

Answer: B

Explanation: The first witch proudly declares, "Here I have a pilot's thumb, Wracked as homeward he did come."

What did Lady Macbeth receive at the beginning of her first scene?

A) A letter from Macbeth

B) A visitor

C) A message from the King

D) A gift

Answer: A

Explanation: The text states, "Lady Macbeth held the letter which the messenger had just brought her."

What news does the servant bring Lady Macbeth?

A) Macbeth has been crowned King

B) The King is coming to visit that night

C) Macbeth has been killed in battle

D) The witches have returned

Answer: B

Explanation: The servant informs Lady Macbeth, "The king comes here tonight."

What does Lady Macbeth say about her role in the "great business"?

A) She will take charge and lead the way

B) She will support Macbeth's decisions

C) She will seek guidance from others

D) She will remain uninvolved

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth tells Macbeth, "You must leave tonight's great business to me."

What does Duncan say about the castle's location as they approach Inverness?

A) It is unpleasantly situated

B) It is pleasantly situated

C) It is too exposed

D) It is too isolated

Answer: B

Explanation: Duncan says, "This castle is pleasantly situated."

What did Malcolm say about Cawdor's execution?

A) Cawdor confessed his treasons and was repentant

B) Cawdor denied the charges and pleaded for his life

C) Cawdor tried to escape and was killed

D) Cawdor's execution was delayed

Answer: A

Explanation: Malcolm says that Cawdor "confessed his treasons very frankly" and was "deeply repentant" before his execution.

What does Lady Macbeth plan to do to the chamber attendants?

A) Bribe them to leave

B) Get them drunk

C) Threaten them

D) Dismiss them

Answer: B

Explanation: Lady Macbeth says, "I'll make sure his two chamber attendants are so drunk that they won't be able to remember a thing."

What news does Ross bring to King Duncan?

A) The defeat of Macdonwald's forces

B) The victory over Norway and the Thane of Cawdor

C) The execution of the Thane of Cawdor

D) The appointment of Macbeth as the new Thane of Cawdor

Answer: B

Explanation: Ross brings the news of the victory over Norway and the Thane of Cawdor, who had betrayed Scotland.

What did the Norwegian king do after Macdonwald's defeat?

A) He retreated

B) He launched a fresh assault with reinforcements

C) He surrendered

D) He sought peace negotiations

Answer: B

Explanation: According to the sergeant, the Norwegian king "began a fresh assault" with reinforcements after Macdonwald's defeat.

What does Lady Macbeth advise Macbeth to do?

A) Act innocent and welcoming

B) Confess his desires to Duncan

C) Flee from the castle

D) Seek guidance from the witches

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth tells Macbeth, "To deceive everyone look like everyone else. Be welcoming: show welcome in your eyes, your hand, your tongue."

How does Duncan describe his trust in Cawdor?

A) He had absolute trust in him

B) He had always doubted his loyalty

C) He had been warned about his treachery

D) He had never fully trusted him

Answer: A

Explanation: Duncan says, "He was a gentleman on whom I had built an absolute trust."

What new title does Duncan bestow upon Malcolm?

A) King of Scotland

B) Prince of Cumberland

C) Duke of Inverness

D) Lord of Cawdor

Answer: B

Explanation: Duncan announces, "We are making our eldest, Malcolm, our heir, and from now on he will be known as the Prince of Cumberland."

What does Banquo say about Macbeth's new honors?

A) They were well-deserved

B) They were like new clothes that take time to fit

C) They would lead to Macbeth's downfall

D) They were a sign of Macbeth's ambition

Answer: B

Explanation: Banquo says to Macbeth, "He's having difficulty with his new honours, Which are like new clothes that don't really fit till we've worn them for a while."

Where did the Norwegian banners fly freely, according to Ross?

A) In Cawdor

B) In Fife

C) In Forres

D) In Norway

Answer: B

Explanation: Ross states that the Norwegian banners had been "flying freely" in Fife.

What did Macbeth do with Macdonwald's head after defeating him?

A) He kept it as a trophy

B) He fixed it on the battlements

C) He buried it with honor

D) He sent it to King Duncan

Answer: B

Explanation: The sergeant reports that Macbeth "fixed his \[Macdonwald's\] head on our battlements" after defeating him.

How does Lady Macbeth greet Duncan and Banquo?

A) She remains inside the castle

B) She runs out to meet them

C) She sends servants to greet them

D) She ignores their arrival

Answer: B

Explanation: The text states, "Lady Macbeth ran out to meet them."

What is the Thane of Cawdor accused of?

A) Cowardice

B) Treason or disloyalty

C) Desertion

D) Insubordination

Answer: B

Explanation: The Thane of Cawdor is described as "that most disloyal of traitors," indicating he is accused of treason or disloyalty against Scotland.

What does Lady Macbeth say about the honors Duncan has bestowed on their house?

A) She is grateful for them

B) She considers them inadequate

C) She is indifferent to them

D) She finds them burdensome

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth says, "For everything you've done in the past and now these new honours, we are deeply in your debt."

How does Lady Macbeth respond when Duncan calls her a "gracious hostess"?

A) She thanks him for the compliment

B) She downplays her role as hostess

C) She asserts her ownership of the castle

D) She declares herself and everything as Duncan's servants

Answer: D

Explanation: Lady Macbeth says, "We are your servants. Everything in our home is yours."

What does Macbeth say when Duncan asks about his whereabouts?

A) He was preparing for the feast

B) He was inspecting the battlements

C) He was meeting with his advisors

D) He was attending to castle business

Answer: A

Explanation: The text indicates that Macbeth tells Duncan he was preparing for the feast.

What emotion does Lady Macbeth ask the spirits to remove from her?

A) Fear

B) Doubt

C) Pity

D) Anger

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth asks the spirits to "stop me from feeling pity" so that her "natural feelings" don't get in the way.

What did the sergeant say about how Macbeth reached Macdonwald during the battle?

A) He charged through the enemy troops

B) He used stealth and surprise tactics

C) He led a cavalry charge

D) He was carried by his soldiers

Answer: A

Explanation: The sergeant states that Macbeth "carved his way through the troops" to reach Macdonwald.

How does the sergeant describe Macbeth and Banquo's reaction to the Norwegian king's fresh assault?

A) They were dismayed

B) They were undaunted, like eagles facing sparrows

C) They fled the battlefield

D) They sought reinforcements

Answer: B

Explanation: The sergeant says that Macbeth and Banquo were undaunted by the fresh assault, comparing their bravery to eagles facing sparrows.

What does Duncan say about the effort put into his entertainment?

A) He is frustrated by the trouble

B) He is indifferent to the effort

C) He appreciates the effort

D) He finds the effort excessive

Answer: C

Explanation: Duncan says, "People take so much trouble over my entertainment. It can be a problem but I do appreciate it."

What do Banquo's observations about the swifts' nests suggest?

A) The air is fresh and delicate

B) The castle is old and decaying

C) The castle is well-guarded

D) The castle is unsafe for birds

Answer: A

Explanation: Banquo says, "I've noticed that wherever they breed the air is delicate."

What does King Duncan decide regarding the Thane of Cawdor?

A) To pardon him

B) To execute him

C) To promote him

D) To exile him

Answer: B

Explanation: King Duncan orders the immediate execution of the Thane of Cawdor for his treachery.

Who is praised for his bravery in the battle?

A) The Thane of Ross

B) King Duncan

C) Macbeth

D) Banquo

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth is praised by the sergeant and Ross for his bravery and valor in the battle against Macdonwald and Norway.

What was the second title the witches addressed Macbeth with?

A) Thane of Glamis

B) Thane of Cawdor

C) King of Scotland

D) None of the above

Answer: B

Explanation: The second witch addresses Macbeth as "Thane of Cawdor" in her greeting.

How does Ross describe the outcome of the battle with Norway?

A) Scotland was defeated

B) The battle was a draw

C) Scotland emerged victorious

D) The battle is still ongoing

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross reports that the victory "fell on us," indicating that Scotland emerged victorious in the battle against Norway.

What does Macbeth ultimately attribute his desire to murder Duncan to?

A) His ambition

B) His fear of the witches' prophecies

C) His hatred for Duncan

D) His desire for revenge

Answer: A

Explanation: The text states that Macbeth has "no excuse - only vaulting ambition, which threatened to overreach itself and bring him crashing down."

Act II

What did Banquo give to Fleance?

A) A sword and a belt

B) A torch and a dagger

C) A diamond and a ring

D) A letter and a key

Answer: A

Explanation: Banquo said, "Take my sword." and later, "Here take this too," referring to his belt.

According to Fleance, what happened to the moon?

A) It was full

B) It was rising

C) It had gone down

D) It was eclipsed

Answer: C

Explanation: Fleance said, "But the moon's gone down."

What was Banquo tired of?

A) Waiting for Macbeth

B) Having horrendous dreams

C) Talking to Fleance

D) Guarding the King

Answer: B

Explanation: Banquo said, "Merciful powers! What horrendous dreams I've been having!"

What did the King give to Banquo for his wife?

A) A sword

B) A diamond

C) A letter

D) A title

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth took a diamond out of his pocket, saying it was from the King for Banquo's wife.

What did Macbeth see in the darkness?

A) Banquo

B) The King

C) A ghost

D) A dagger

Answer: D

Explanation: As Macbeth stared into the darkness, it seemed that a dagger hung there.

What appeared on the dagger that wasn't there before?

A) Rust

B) Blood

C) Inscriptions

D) Jewels

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth noticed that there was now blood on the dagger, which hadn't been there before.

What brought Macbeth back to the business at hand?

A) Banquo's voice

B) The King's snoring

C) A bell ringing

D) His servant's return

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth was overwhelmed by images of fear and horror until a bell rang and brought him back to the business at hand.

What had Lady Macbeth drunk that filled her with fire?

A) Water

B) Tea

C) Wine

D) Milk

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth had drunk some wine, and it had filled her with fire.

What had Lady Macbeth laid out for Macbeth?

A) Swords

B) Poison

C) Daggers

D) Ropes

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth had laid out the daggers, ready for Macbeth.

Why didn't Lady Macbeth kill Duncan herself?

A) She was afraid

B) She didn't have a weapon

C) Duncan looked too much like her father as he slept

D) She wanted Macbeth to do it

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth would have killed Duncan herself if he hadn't looked so much like her father as he slept.

Who was sleeping in the room next to Duncan's?

A) Banquo

B) Fleance

C) Donalbain

D) Malcolm

Answer: C

Explanation: When Macbeth asked who was sleeping in the room next to Duncan's, Lady Macbeth replied, "Donalbain."

What did Macbeth say about his hands?

A) They were clean

B) They were dirty

C) They were a sorry sight

D) They were bleeding

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth peered at his hands in the dim light of his wife's torch and said, "This is a sorry sight."

What did Lady Macbeth tell Macbeth to do with the daggers?

A) Hide them

B) Clean them

C) Take them back and smear the grooms with blood

D) Throw them away

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth told Macbeth to take the daggers back and smear the grooms with blood.

Where was the knocking coming from?

A) The north entry

B) The south entry

C) The east entry

D) The west entry

Answer: B

Explanation: Lady Macbeth said there was someone knocking at the south entry.

What did Lady Macbeth tell Macbeth to do after washing his hands?

A) Go to sleep

B) Hide the evidence

C) Put on his nightgown

D) Answer the door

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth told Macbeth to put on his nightgown so it would seem as though they had been to bed.

What did the porter mutter to himself as he staggered out?

A) "This is the gate of heaven."

B) "This is the gate of purgatory."

C) "This is the gate of hell."

D) "This is the gate of the castle."

Answer: C

Explanation: The porter muttered, "This is the gate of hell, this is."

According to the porter, what does drink do to you?

A) It makes you wise

B) It makes you rich

C) It makes you healthy

D) It makes your nose red, makes you sleep, and makes you piss

Answer: D

Explanation: The porter said that drink "makes your nose red, it makes you sleep and it makes you piss."

Who arrived at the castle gate?

A) Macduff and Lennox

B) Banquo and Fleance

C) Malcolm and Donalbain

D) Macbeth and Lady Macbeth

Answer: A

Explanation: Macduff, the Thane of Fife, accompanied by Lennox, arrived at the castle gate.

What did Lennox say about the previous night?

A) It was a calm night

B) It was a wild night

C) It was a cold night

D) It was a hot night

Answer: B

Explanation: Lennox said, "It was a wild night where we stayed."

What happened to the chimneys, according to Lennox?

A) They were cleaned

B) They were blown down

C) They were on fire

D) They were rebuilt

Answer: B

Explanation: Lennox said, "Our chimneys were blown down."

What did Macbeth say about his love for Duncan?

A) It was weaker than his reason

B) It was equal to his reason

C) It was stronger than his reason

D) He didn't love Duncan at all

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth said, "My love for royal Duncan was stronger than my reason."

What did Macbeth say about Duncan's appearance?

A) His golden skin was laced with his silver blood

B) His silver skin was laced with his golden blood

C) His skin was pale and his blood was red

D) His skin was dark and his blood was black

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth said, "Here lay Duncan, his silver skin laced with his golden blood."

What happened to Lady Macbeth during the commotion?

A) She fainted

B) She screamed

C) She ran away

D) She accused someone of the murder

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth moaned, thrust her hand up dramatically to her forehead, said "Help me!" and dropped down.

Where did Malcolm say he was going?

A) Ireland

B) England

C) Wales

D) Scotland

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm said, "I'm going to England."

Where did Donalbain say he was going?

A) Ireland

B) England

C) Wales

D) Scotland

Answer: A

Explanation: Donalbain said, "Ireland for me."

Where was Ross when he heard the news of Duncan's death?

A) At his own castle

B) At Macbeth's castle

C) At the castle of a friend

D) At Duncan's castle

Answer: C

Explanation: The news of Duncan's death came to Ross at the castle of a friend.

What was Inverness almost completely hidden by?

A) Fog

B) Smoke

C) Clouds

D) Darkness

Answer: A

Explanation: Inverness was almost completely hidden by the black fog.

Who did Ross meet on his way to the castle gates?

A) A young man

B) A soldier

C) An old man

D) A priest

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross drew alongside an old man who was labouring uphill, puffing and gasping.

What time of day was it according to the clock?

A) Night

B) Morning

C) Afternoon

D) Daytime

Answer: D

Explanation: Ross said, "It's daytime, according to the clock, but darkness strangles the sun."

What happened to a falcon last Tuesday, according to the old man?

A) It was killed by its prey

B) It was killed by a mousing hawk

C) It was killed by a hunter

D) It died of natural causes

Answer: B

Explanation: The old man said, "Last Tuesday a falcon, surveying its prey, was hunted by a mousing hawk and killed."

What happened to Duncan's horses?

A) They were stolen

B) They were killed

C) They went wild and broke out of their stalls

D) They disappeared

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross said, "And I hear Duncan's horses, the most perfect examples of their type, went wild, broke out of their stalls and refused to be controlled."

According to the old man, what did Duncan's horses do?

A) They ran away

B) They killed each other

C) They ate each other

D) They attacked people

Answer: C

Explanation: The old man said, "They say they ate each other!"

Who did Macduff say had killed Duncan?

A) Malcolm and Donalbain

B) The King's grooms

C) Macbeth

D) Banquo

Answer: B

Explanation: Macduff said, "The ones Macbeth killed. Duncan's grooms."

What did Macduff say about Malcolm and Donalbain?

A) They had been killed

B) They had been arrested

C) They had stolen away and fled

D) They had been crowned as kings

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff said, "Malcolm and Donalbain have stolen away and fled. Which places suspicion on them."

Where had Macbeth gone?

A) To England

B) To Ireland

C) To Scone to be crowned

D) To Fife

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth had gone to Scone to be crowned.

Where was Duncan's body taken?

A) To Macbeth's castle

B) To Scone

C) To Colme-kiln

D) To England

Answer: C

Explanation: Duncan's body was carried to Colme-kiln, the sacred resting place of kings.

Where was Macduff going?

A) To Scone

B) To England

C) Home to Fife

D) To Macbeth's castle

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff said, "No, Cousin, I'm going home to Fife."

What did the old man say to Ross as he rode off?

A) "Goodbye and good luck"

B) "God's blessing be with you"

C) "Be careful out there"

D) "See you soon"

Answer: B

Explanation: The old man said, "God's blessing be with you."

Who did Macduff tell to ring the alarm bell?

A) The porter

B) The servants

C) Everyone

D) The guards

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff shouted, "Wake up! Wake up! Ring the alarm bell! Murder and treason!"

Who said Duncan's grooms were covered in blood?

A) Macbeth

B) Lady Macbeth

C) Lennox

D) Banquo

Answer: C

Explanation: Lennox said, "His chamberlains, their hands and faces were all smeared with blood."

Act III

Where are Macbeth's "murderous cousins" said to be hiding?

A) England and Ireland

B) Scotland and Wales

C) France and Spain

D) Norway and Denmark

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth states "We hear our murderous cousins are hiding in England and Ireland".

What time is the formal banquet scheduled for?

A) Six o'clock

B) Seven o'clock

C) Eight o'clock

D) Nine o'clock

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth tells his courtiers "Find ways of amusing yourselves till seven tonight".

How many men does Macbeth meet with to discuss Banquo?

A) One

B) Two

C) Three

D) Four

Answer: B

Explanation: The text mentions "The attendant returned with two rough-looking men".

What does Lady Macbeth feel about gaining something without it bringing happiness?

A) It is a waste

B) It is a blessing

C) It is a challenge

D) It is a mystery

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth thinks "we gain nothing and waste everything when we get what we want without it bringing us happiness."

What did Macbeth compare their situation to, regarding the person they killed?

A) They have killed the lion, not the cub

B) They have wounded the snake, not killed it

C) They have cut down the tree, not the branches

D) They have silenced the witness, not the crime

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth states "We've wounded the snake, not killed it, And it will recover and bite us."

What does Macbeth say Duncan is enjoying in his grave?

A) Wealth

B) Fame

C) Power

D) Peace

Answer: D

Explanation: Macbeth says "Duncan's in his grave. After the fever of life he sleeps well."

What does Macbeth say his mind is full of?

A) Doubts

B) Fears

C) Scorpions

D) Regrets

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth exclaims "Oh, my mind is full of scorpions."

When does Macbeth say the dreadful thing will happen to Banquo and Fleance?

A) By dawn

B) By noon

C) By nightfall

D) By midnight

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth specifies that the dreadful thing will happen to Banquo and Fleance "by nightfall."

What does Banquo shout to the grooms as he approaches?

A) "Bring me a horse!"

B) "Bring me a light!"

C) "Bring me my sword!"

D) "Bring me my cloak!"

Answer: B

Explanation: Banquo's voice is heard shouting "Bring me a light!"

What does Banquo say as he is being attacked?

A) "Oh treachery! Run, Fleance, run, run."

B) "Oh you devils! I will fight you!"

C) "Oh help! Someone help me!"

D) "Oh no! I am doomed!"

Answer: A

Explanation: Banquo gasps "Oh treachery. Run, Fleance, run, run. Oh you devil."

What happens to Fleance during the attack?

A) He is killed

B) He is captured

C) He disappears into the dark woods

D) He fights off the murderers

Answer: C

Explanation: The murderers chase after Fleance but "he disappeared into the dark woods."

Where does the banquet scene take place?

A) In the courtyard

B) In the great hall

C) In the state dining room

D) In the royal bedchamber

Answer: C

Explanation: The scene is set in "the huge doors of the glittering state dining room."

Who is in attendance at the banquet?

A) Only the thanes

B) Only the lords and ladies

C) Everyone of importance in Scotland

D) Only Macbeth's closest friends

Answer: C

Explanation: The text states "everyone of importance in Scotland had been commanded to attend - from the great thanes down to the lesser lords and their ladies."

What does Macbeth notice about the murderer's appearance?

A) He is wearing a disguise

B) He has a wound on his face

C) He has blood on his face

D) He looks frightened

Answer: C

Explanation: When Macbeth approaches the murderer, he says "There's blood on your face!"

What does the murderer say he did to Banquo?

A) Stabbed him in the heart

B) Cut his throat

C) Shot him with an arrow

D) Strangled him

Answer: B

Explanation: The murderer says "My Lord, his throat is cut. I did that for him."

How does the murderer describe Banquo's condition?

A) Quite safe, asleep in his bed

B) Quite safe, hiding in the woods

C) Quite safe, buried in a ditch with twenty deep gashes in his head

D) Quite safe, on his way to England

Answer: C

Explanation: The murderer says "Yes, my Lord, quite safe, buried in a ditch with twenty deep gashes in his head."

How does Lady Macbeth want Macbeth to treat Banquo at the banquet?

A) As a special guest

B) As a potential threat

C) As a loyal friend

D) As a distant acquaintance

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth instructs Macbeth to treat Banquo "as a special guest - in every way."

What is Inverness almost completely hidden by?

A) Fog

B) Smoke

C) Clouds

D) Darkness

Answer: A

Explanation: Inverness was almost completely hidden by the black fog.

According to the old man, what did Duncan's horses do?

A) They ran away

B) They killed each other

C) They ate each other

D) They attacked people

Answer: C

Explanation: The old man said, "They say they ate each other!"

What happened to a falcon last Tuesday, according to the old man?

A) It was killed by its prey

B) It was killed by a mousing hawk

C) It was killed by a hunter

D) It died of natural causes

Answer: B

Explanation: The old man said, "Last Tuesday a falcon, surveying its prey, was hunted by a mousing hawk and killed."

Where had Macbeth gone?

A) To England

B) To Ireland

C) To Scone to be crowned

D) To Fife

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth had gone to Scone to be crowned.

Where was Duncan's body taken?

A) To Macbeth's castle

B) To Scone

C) To Colme-kiln

D) To England

Answer: C

Explanation: Duncan's body was carried to Colme-kiln, the sacred resting place of kings.

What does Lady Macbeth say will make Macbeth's condition worse?

A) Ignoring him

B) Giving him too much attention

C) Trying to reason with him

D) Calling for a doctor

Answer: B

Explanation: Lady Macbeth instructs the guests "if you give it too much attention it will make him worse."

What does Macbeth say they should do with the dead if they won't stay in their graves?

A) Bury them deeper

B) Burn their bodies

C) Feed them to birds of prey

D) Seal their tombs with holy water

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth declares "If the dead won't stay in their graves then we should feed them to birds of prey."

What dangerous animals does Macbeth say he would dare to face?

A) Lions, tigers, and bears

B) Russian bear, armour plated rhinoceros, Hyrcan tiger

C) Wolves, snakes, and dragons

D) Crocodiles, sharks, and eagles

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth declares "Come at me like the rugged Russian bear, or the armour plated rhinoceros, or the Hyrcan tiger."

What does Macbeth say Banquo's bones and blood are like?

A) Marrowless and cold

B) Strong and hot

C) Decayed and putrid

D) Dry and brittle

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth shouts at the apparition "Your bones are marrowless, your blood is cold."

What does Lady Macbeth say adds flavor to the meat at a banquet?

A) Good company

B) Fine wine

C) Ceremony

D) Music and dancing

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macbeth says "Ceremony adds flavour to the meat: without it it's tasteless."

What happens to Macbeth when he looks at the empty chair reserved for him?

A) He faints

B) He starts laughing hysterically

C) He becomes angry

D) He sways and loses color in his cheeks

Answer: D

Explanation: When Macbeth looks at the empty chair, "All the colour in his cheeks drained away. He swayed."

According to Lady Macbeth, how long has Macbeth been having these fits?

A) Since childhood

B) Since becoming king

C) Since murdering Duncan

D) Since meeting the weird sisters

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth tells the guests that Macbeth "is often like this - has been since childhood."

What does Macbeth say about his love for Duncan?

A) It was weaker than his reason

B) It was equal to his reason

C) It was stronger than his reason

D) He didn't love Duncan at all

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth said, "My love for royal Duncan was stronger than my reason."

According to the murderer, how many of Banquo's wounds would have been enough to kill him?

A) None of them

B) Five of them

C) Ten of them

D) Each one of them

Answer: D

Explanation: The murderer specifies that each one of the twenty gashes "would have been enough to kill him."

What does Banquo say just before he is attacked?

A) "It's a beautiful evening."

B) "I hope we get there soon."

C) "Looks like rain tonight."

D) "I think we're being followed."

Answer: C

Explanation: Just before the attack, Banquo's voice comes out of the dark saying "Looks like rain tonight."

What does Lady Macbeth say Macbeth has ruined with his loss of control?

A) The atmosphere and the dinner

B) Their reputation and their marriage

C) Their chances of keeping the throne

D) The trust of their friends and allies

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macbeth whispers to Macbeth "You've ruined the atmosphere and destroyed the dinner with an astonishing loss of control."

According to Macbeth, what have been known to tell on murderers?

A) Ghosts and spirits

B) Gravestones and trees

C) Birds and animals

D) The wind and the rain

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth says "Gravestones have been known to tell on murderers and trees to speak their names."

When does Macbeth plan to find the weird sisters?

A) Early tomorrow morning

B) At midnight

C) In a fortnight

D) On the next full moon

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth declares "I'm going to find the weird sisters early tomorrow morning."

What does Macbeth say he has waded so far into that he couldn't turn back now?

A) Lies

B) Ambition

C) Blood

D) Prophecy

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth reflects "I've waded so far into blood that I couldn't turn back now."

What does Macbeth attribute his episode at the banquet to?

A) The fear that inexperience in evil brings

B) The guilt of a heavy conscience

C) The stress of being a new king

D) The influence of supernatural forces

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth concludes "This episode tonight is the fear that inexperience in evil brings."

Where does Macbeth say he will sit during the banquet?

A) At the head of the table

B) Beside his queen

C) Among the guests

D) He will not sit down

Answer: C

Explanation: Macbeth states "Ourself will mingle with you and play the humble host."

What does Macbeth say about his state of mind before hearing the news about Fleance?

A) As firm as marble, as solid as rock, as free as air

B) As shaky as jelly, as fragile as glass, as trapped as a prisoner

C) As calm as a still pond, as clear as a blue sky, as light as a feather

D) As dark as night, as heavy as lead, as cold as ice

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth felt "as firm as marble, as solid as rock, as free as air" before the news.

What does Macbeth do with the chalice after toasting Banquo?

A) Drinks from it

B) Passes it to Lady Macbeth

C) Sets it down gently

D) Flings it across the room

Answer: D

Explanation: After toasting Banquo, Macbeth "flung the chalice across the room. It landed with a mighty clatter on the stone floor."

Act IV

Where are the weird sisters gathered?

A) In a dark cave

B) In a lonely heath

C) In a forest

D) In a castle

Answer: A

Explanation: The text mentions that the weird sisters have gathered in a dark cave deep below the mud and slime of a lonely heath.

What is covering the table?

A) Fresh herbs

B) Foul-smelling, disgusting items

C) Magical potions

D) Rare spices

Answer: B

Explanation: The text describes the table as being covered with foul-smelling, disgusting items.

How many times did the brindled cat mew?

A) Once

B) Twice

C) Thrice

D) Four times

Answer: C

Explanation: One of the weird sisters says, "Thrice the brindled cat hath mewed."

How many days and nights did the toad swelter venom under a cold stone?

A) Twenty-one

B) Thirty

C) Thirty-one

D) Forty-one

Answer: C

Explanation: The text says, "Toad, that under cold stone Days and nights has thirty-one Sweltered venom, sleeping got."

What is slivered in the moon's eclipse?

A) Gall of goat

B) Slips of yew

C) Nose of Turk

D) Tartar's lips

Answer: B

Explanation: The text lists "Gall of goat, and slips of yew Slivered in the moon's eclipse" as ingredients.

Who arrives with a resounding thundercrack?

A) Macbeth

B) Hecate

C) The three weird sisters

D) Elves and fairies

Answer: B

Explanation: The text mentions that a resounding thundercrack brings Hecate, riding on its back.

What appears before Macbeth in the featureless place?

A) A head wearing a helmet

B) A blood-smeared child

C) A child with a crown and a tree branch

D) The three weird sisters

Answer: A

Explanation: The text describes a head wearing a helmet hanging in the air before Macbeth in the featureless place.

What does the head wearing a helmet tell Macbeth?

A) To beware Macduff, the Thane of Fife

B) To be bloody, bold, and resolute

C) To laugh at the power of man

D) That none of woman born shall harm him

Answer: A

Explanation: The head wearing a helmet cries, "Macbeth! Macbeth! Macbeth! Beware Macduff: beware the Thane of Fife."

What does the blood-smeared child tell Macbeth?

A) To beware Macduff, the Thane of Fife

B) To be bloody, bold, and resolute

C) To laugh at the power of man

D) That none of woman born shall harm him

Answer: B

Explanation: The blood-smeared child says, "Be bloody, bold and resolute: laugh to scorn The power of man, for none of woman born Shall harm Macbeth."

Where has Lady Macduff's husband fled to?

A) Scotland

B) Wales

C) Ireland

D) England

Answer: D

Explanation: Lady Macduff says, "What's he done, that he's had to flee to England?"

What does Lady Macduff say even the smallest of birds will do?

A) Flee from danger

B) Abandon their young

C) Stay and fight an owl to save their chickens

D) Seek help from other birds

Answer: C

Explanation: Lady Macduff says, "Even the tiny wren, the smallest of birds will stay and fight an owl to save her chickens."

Who bursts into the room while Lady Macduff is talking with her son?

A) Macbeth

B) Ross

C) A travel-worn man

D) Two murderers

Answer: C

Explanation: The text states, "The door burst open and a travel-worn man came in."

What does the travel-worn man tell Lady Macduff?

A) She is in immediate danger and must leave

B) Her husband has been killed

C) Macbeth has been overthrown

D) The war is over and they are safe

Answer: A

Explanation: The travel-worn man says, "Listen, I haven't got much time. You're in immediate danger. You must leave right now."

What does Lady Macduff's son call the man who threatens them?

A) A liar

B) A shaggy-haired lout

C) A coward

D) A traitor

Answer: B

Explanation: The boy says, "You lie, you shaggy-haired lout!"

What are Lady Macduff's last words as she runs?

A) "Help! Murder! Help! My babies!"

B) "Run for your lives! Murderers!"

C) "My son! My poor son!"

D) "Traitors! Villains! Monsters!"

Answer: A

Explanation: As she runs, Lady Macduff cries, "Help! Murder! Help! My babies!"

Where does Malcolm usher Macduff to?

A) A garden at King Edward's palace

B) A war room in the castle

C) A dungeon beneath the palace

D) A busy marketplace in the city

Answer: A

Explanation: The text states, "Malcolm ushered Macduff out into a garden at King Edward's palace."

What has King Edward of England offered to Malcolm?

A) Sanctuary in his palace

B) His daughter's hand in marriage

C) Thousands of men to help regain his rights

D) A peace treaty with Scotland

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm mentions, "And here in England, gracious Edward has offered me thousands of men."

Who is setting out with ten thousand men?

A) Macduff

B) Malcolm

C) Old Siward

D) Ross

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm tells Macduff, "Even at this moment, Old Siward is setting out with ten thousand men."

How does Ross describe the current state of Scotland?

A) Peaceful and prosperous

B) Slowly recovering from turmoil

C) Almost afraid to know itself, like a grave where no one smiles

D) United against Macbeth's tyranny

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross says, "Alas, poor country! Almost afraid to know itself. It can't be called our mother, but our grave - where no-one ever smiles anymore."

What does Ross say about the lifespan of good men in Scotland?

A) Longer than ever before

B) Equal to the lifespan of flowers in their hats

C) Shorter than the flowers they wear in their hats

D) Unaffected by the current situation

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross says, "good men's lives are shorter than the flowers they wear in their hats - dying even before they begin to fade!"

What happened to Macduff's castle?

A) It was destroyed by fire

B) It was seized by Macbeth's men

C) It was surprised and attacked

D) It was abandoned by his family

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross says, "Your castle was surprised; your wife and babes savagely slaughtered."

Who was killed in the attack on Macduff's castle?

A) Only his servants

B) Only his wife

C) Only his children

D) His wife, children, and everyone they could find

Answer: D

Explanation: When Macduff asks, "My children too?" Ross responds, "Wife, servants, everyone they could find."

What does Malcolm say about his alleged vices?

A) They are all true

B) They are only partially true

C) He exaggerated them to test Macduff

D) He is not like that at all

Answer: D

Explanation: Malcolm says, "I'm not like that at all. I've never been with a woman; I've never perjured myself, hardly ever coveted something that wasn't my own and never broken a promise."

What does Macduff say about Macbeth after learning of his family's murder?

A) "He has no children"

B) "He is a coward and a traitor"

C) "He will suffer for this in hell"

D) "He is no longer human to me"

Answer: A

Explanation: In his grief, Macduff says, "He has no children," referring to Macbeth.

How does Malcolm tell Macduff to handle his grief?

A) "Take it like a man"

B) "Weep and mourn for as long as you need"

C) "Bury your sorrow and focus on revenge"

D) "Leave the fighting to me and go to safety"

Answer: A

Explanation: Malcolm says to Macduff, "Take it like a man."

Who does Macduff blame for the death of his family?

A) Macbeth

B) Malcolm

C) Himself

D) Ross

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff says, "Oh, it's my fault. They were killed because of me! Not because of their faults but for mine."

What does the child with a crown and a tree branch tell Macbeth?

A) To beware Macduff, the Thane of Fife

B) To be bloody, bold, and resolute

C) To laugh at the power of man

D) That Macbeth shall never be vanquished until Birnam Wood comes to Dunsinane Hill

Answer: D

Explanation: The child with a crown and a tree branch says, "Macbeth shall never vanquished be, until Great Birnam Wood to high Dunsinane Hill shall come against him."

What does Macbeth say about the prophecy regarding Birnam Wood?

A) It's impossible because trees can't walk

B) It's a warning that he should heed

C) It's a sign that he will be victorious

D) It's a riddle that he must solve

Answer: A

Explanation: Macbeth says, "That's impossible. Trees can't walk. Birnam wood will never come to the castle at Dunsinane!"

What does Malcolm say about the times they are living in?

A) They are prosperous

B) They are bad

C) They are confusing

D) They are changing

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm says "the times are bad when the most loyal people are accused of being traitors."

What does Ross say about taking notice of torture in Scotland?

A) It happens rarely

B) People protest against it

C) No one takes any notice of it anymore

D) It only happens to criminals

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross says "where no-one takes any notice of the groaning and shrieking of torture."

How does Ross initially respond when Macduff asks about his wife?

A) He says she is dead

B) He says she is well

C) He says she is in hiding

D) He refuses to answer

Answer: B

Explanation: When Macduff asks, "How's my wife?" Ross hesitates before saying, "Why . . . she's well."

What does Ross say would happen if Malcolm returned to Scotland?

A) The people would flee

B) Macbeth would surrender

C) Even the women would fight

D) The nobles would resist

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross tells Malcolm, "Your presence in Scotland would inspire everyone to fight. Even our women would respond."

What does Macduff want to do when he meets Macbeth?

A) Reason with him

B) Forgive him

C) Bring him within his sword's length

D) Capture him alive

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff says, "Bring this fiend of Scotland and me face to face. Bring him within my sword's length."

What does Malcolm say about Macduff's grief?

A) It should turn to anger

B) It should be forgotten

C) It should be hidden

D) It should consume him

Answer: A

Explanation: Malcolm says, "Let grief turn into anger."

What does Lady Macduff say about her son after Ross calls him "pretty cousin"?

A) He is brave

B) He is an orphan even though he has a father

C) He is too young to understand

D) He will grow up to be like his father

Answer: B

Explanation: Lady Macduff says, "He's an orphan even though he has a father."

What does Ross say is the state of Scotland under Macbeth's rule?

A) It is prospering

B) It is at peace

C) It is like a grave where no one smiles

D) It is preparing for war

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross describes Scotland as "our grave - where no-one ever smiles anymore."

What does Malcolm say about Macbeth's current state?

A) He is stronger than ever

B) He is weak and vulnerable

C) He is ripe for shaking

D) He is ready to surrender

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm says, "Macbeth is ripe for shaking."

What does Macduff say about escaping Macbeth?

A) He will pursue him to the ends of the earth

B) He will accept it as God's will

C) He will take his own life in shame

D) He will let heaven forgive him

Answer: D

Explanation: Macduff says, "If he escapes let my punishment be that heaven forgives him."

What does Lady Macduff compare her husband's absence to?

A) A betrayal of his family

B) A necessary sacrifice

C) An act of cowardice

D) An act of wisdom

Answer: A

Explanation: Lady Macduff says, "He doesn't love us. Where's his fatherly instinct?"

What news does Ross say he has for Macduff?

A) It is the best news he has ever heard

B) It is of little importance

C) It concerns the political situation only

D) It is the worst news Macduff has ever heard

Answer: D

Explanation: Ross tells Macduff, "Don't hate me for ever, because this is the worst news you've ever heard."

Act V

Where was the King conducting the war from?

A) His northern castle at Dunsinane

B) His palace in the capital

C) A military encampment in the field

D) An undisclosed location

Answer: A

Explanation: The passage states that "the King to retreat to his northern castle at Dunsinane and it was from there that he was conducting the war."

What did the doctor notice about the queen's eyes during her sleepwalking?

A) They were closed

B) They were open but unfocused

C) They were darting around rapidly

D) They were filled with tears

Answer: B

Explanation: The doctor states "You see? Her eyes are open."

What did the queen say about Banquo being buried?

A) Banquo is not really dead

B) Banquo's grave needs to be dug up

C) Banquo's buried and can't come out

D) Banquo's murder should be kept secret

Answer: C

Explanation: The queen states "Banquo's buried: he can't come out of his grave."

What did the doctor say the queen had more need of than a doctor?

A) A priest

B) A psychiatrist

C) A personal assistant

D) A bodyguard

Answer: A

Explanation: The doctor states "She has more need of a priest than a doctor."

What did the gentlewoman say she wouldn't want to have in her bosom?

A) The queen's crown

B) The queen's jewels

C) The queen's heart

D) The queen's handkerchief

Answer: C

Explanation: The gentlewoman says "I wouldn't have her heart in my bosom for anything."

What did the doctor instruct the gentlewoman to do?

A) Keep the queen under constant watch

B) Lock the queen in her room for her own safety

C) Give the queen sleeping medication

D) Summon the priest to speak with the queen

Answer: A

Explanation: The doctor tells the gentlewoman "Look after her. Take away anything that she could use to harm herself with. And don't take your eyes off her."

What did the queen say about the perfumes of Arabia?

A) They could make her hands smell sweet

B) They couldn't make her hands smell sweet

C) They were too expensive to obtain

D) They reminded her of Duncan

Answer: B

Explanation: The queen says "All the perfumes of Arabia couldn't make this little hand smell sweet."

Who had joined the rebels as they advanced?

A) The nobility of England

B) The common people of Scotland

C) The most powerful thanes in Scotland

D) The army of the rightful king

Answer: C

Explanation: The passage states that "as the rebels had advanced they had been joined by some of the most powerful thanes in Scotland."

Where were the rebels planning to meet the English forces?

A) Dunsinane

B) Birnam Wood

C) The king's northern castle

D) An open field

Answer: B

Explanation: The passage states that "We'll meet them near Birnam Wood."

What did Macbeth say about anyone who talked of defeat?

A) They would be rewarded

B) They would be hanged

C) They would be exiled

D) They would be imprisoned

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth orders "Search the countryside: hang anyone who talks of defeat."

What did Malcolm order the soldiers to do to conceal their numbers?

A) Wear dark cloaks

B) Put on branches from Birnam Wood

C) Spread out in a wide formation

D) Disguise themselves as local villagers

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm orders "Tell every soldier to cut down a branch and carry it in front of him. That will conceal our numbers."

What was Macbeth's response to the news of the queen's death?

A) He was devastated and mourned her deeply

B) He was indifferent and felt it was not the right time

C) He was relieved that she was no longer suffering

D) He blamed himself for her death

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth says "She should have died later. This isn't the time."

What did the soldier initially hesitate to tell Macbeth?

A) The enemy forces were approaching

B) Birnam Wood appeared to be moving

C) The queen had died

D) The battle was lost

Answer: B

Explanation: The soldier says "I want to tell you what I think I saw but don't know how to say it."

What did Macbeth say he would do to the soldier if he was lying?

A) Execute him immediately

B) Hang him alive until he starved

C) Send him to fight on the front lines

D) Banish him from Scotland

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth threatens to "string you up on the nearest tree. And you'll hang there, alive, until you shrivel up with starvation."

Who did Malcolm say would lead the first onslaught?

A) Macduff

B) Siward and his son

C) Malcolm himself

D) The English forces

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm states "You'll lead the first onslaught, Uncle - you and your noble son."

What happened to Young Siward when he fought Macbeth?

A) He wounded Macbeth

B) He was killed by Macbeth

C) He retreated from the fight

D) He captured Macbeth

Answer: B

Explanation: The text states that "before long he was lying at Macbeth's feet, his heart pierced by a king's sword."

What was Macduff searching for on the battlefield?

A) His family

B) Macbeth

C) The English forces

D) Malcolm

Answer: B

Explanation: The passage states that Macduff was "looking desperately for Macbeth."

What did Macduff reveal about his birth that negated Macbeth's prophecy?

A) He was born in England

B) He was born by C-section

C) He was born under a special star

D) He was born to a different mother

Answer: B

Explanation: Macduff says he was "pulled prematurely from his mother's womb."

What did Macduff do with Macbeth's head after killing him?

A) Buried it with his body

B) Displayed it on a pike

C) Took it to Malcolm

D) Threw it into the sea

Answer: B

Explanation: Macduff "took a spear from a sentry, pierced the head and raised it up above the battlements."

What news did Ross bring to Siward about his son?

A) His son had been captured

B) His son had been wounded

C) His son had been killed

D) His son had fled

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross tells Siward "Your son's been killed my Lord."

How did Siward react to the news of his son's death?

A) With great sorrow

B) With stoic pride

C) With anger

D) With disbelief

Answer: B

Explanation: Siward says "Well then, he's God's soldier. And so that's the end of it."

Where did Malcolm say his coronation would take place?

A) Dunsinane

B) Edinburgh

C) Scone

D) Inverness

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm invites everyone "to see us crowned at Scone."

What new title did Malcolm give to his thanes?

A) Dukes

B) Lords

C) Earls

D) Barons

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm says "you are now earls - the first earldoms Scotland has ever awarded."

What did Macbeth say about his mental state after years of violence?

A) He had forgotten what fear felt like

B) He had become more cautious

C) He had grown stronger

D) He had become more merciful

Answer: A

Explanation: The passage states that Macbeth "had almost forgotten what fear was like."

What metaphor did Macbeth use to describe life?

A) A rushing river

B) A walking shadow

C) A burning flame

D) A growing tree

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth describes life as "a walking shadow."

What did Malcolm say he would do about the exiles?

A) Execute them

B) Pardon them

C) Call them home

D) Banish them permanently

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm states he will "call our exiled friends home."

What did Ross say about the state of Scotland under Macbeth?

A) It was prospering

B) It was at peace

C) It was like a grave

D) It was united

Answer: C

Explanation: Ross describes Scotland as "our grave - where no-one ever smiles anymore."

What did Macbeth say about Birnam Wood when first told it was moving?

A) He accepted it as true

B) He called the messenger a liar

C) He ignored the warning

D) He surrendered immediately

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth calls the soldier a "Liar!" and a "Rogue!"

How did Malcolm say they should conceal their numbers?

A) By marching at night

B) By carrying branches

C) By splitting into groups

D) By wearing disguises

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm orders soldiers to "cut down a branch and carry it in front of him. That will conceal our numbers."

What did Macduff say about his revenge against Macbeth?

A) He would share it with others

B) He wanted it only for himself

C) He would let Malcolm handle it

D) He would forget about it

Answer: B

Explanation: The passage states that Macduff couldn't let Macbeth be killed by anyone else, as "his murdered wife and children's souls would never rest."

What did Macbeth say about his castle's defenses?

A) They were weak

B) They would mock any siege

C) They needed repair

D) They were abandoned

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth says "Our fortifications will make a mockery of any siege."

What did the doctor say about Lady Macbeth's condition?

A) It was curable with medicine

B) It was beyond his help

C) It would pass with time

D) It was contagious

Answer: B

Explanation: The doctor states that Lady Macbeth "has more need of a priest than a doctor."

What did Malcolm say about rewarding his supporters?

A) He would take his time

B) He would not waste time

C) He would be selective

D) He would forget them

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm says "We won't waste much time in rewarding each and every one of you."

What did Siward say about the battle's casualties?

A) They were too high

B) They were surprisingly light

C) They were unavoidable

D) They were unnecessary

Answer: B

Explanation: Siward says "For such a famous victory I think we've been let off lightly."

How did Macbeth react when Macduff revealed he was not "born of woman"?

A) He surrendered immediately

B) He became enraged

C) He tried to flee

D) He laughed it off

Answer: B

Explanation: The passage states that Macbeth "whipped round" after hearing Macduff's revelation.

What did Lady Macbeth say about the marks on her hands?

A) They would never wash away

B) They were imaginary

C) They would heal

D) They were battle scars

Answer: A

Explanation: The queen says that all the perfumes of Arabia couldn't make her hands smell sweet, indicating she believes the marks will never wash away.

What did Malcolm say about the English support they received?

A) It was insufficient

B) It was crucial

C) It was unexpected

D) It was unwanted

Answer: B

Explanation: Malcolm mentions the importance of "gracious England" and their support in the fight.

What was Macduff's proclamation when he presented Macbeth's head?

A) "Victory is ours!"

B) "The tyrant is dead!"

C) "Hail, King of Scotland!"

D) "Justice is done!"

Answer: C

Explanation: Macduff says "Hail, King, because that's what you are."

What did Macbeth say about fighting to the end?

A) He would surrender

B) He would fight on

C) He would flee

D) He would negotiate

Answer: B

Explanation: Macbeth declares "Even though Birnam Wood has come to Dunsinane and I'm facing a man not born of woman I'll fight to the bitter end."

Where did Malcolm say he would perform his duties as king?

A) Immediately

B) In another country

C) In due course

D) Never

Answer: C

Explanation: Malcolm states "these things, and whatever else God calls on us to do we'll perform in due course."

# Cry, the Peacock – Anita Desai

Author: Anita Desai
Publication Year: 1963

Background

Anita Desai’s Cry, the Peacock is a landmark novel in Indian English literature, first published in 1963. It is Desai’s debut novel and showcases her distinctive style, which blends psychological depth with lyrical prose. The novel is a pioneering work in its exploration of the inner lives of its characters, particularly the protagonist, Maya, whose mental and emotional turmoil forms the crux of the narrative.

Set in post-independence India, Cry, the Peacock delves into themes of alienation, marital discord, and psychological instability. It is a deeply introspective novel that examines the complexities of human relationships, the burden of societal expectations, and the fragility of the human mind. Desai’s focus on the inner world of her characters marked a departure from the predominantly social and political concerns of earlier Indian English writers, establishing her as a key figure in the development of Indian psychological fiction.

Structure

Form: Prose novel with a stream-of-consciousness narrative style.

Chapters: The novel is divided into 12 chapters, though the narrative flows seamlessly, often blurring the boundaries between past and present.

Perspective: The story is told primarily from the first-person perspective of Maya, the protagonist, allowing readers to experience her thoughts, emotions, and descent into madness.

The fragmented and lyrical structure of the novel mirrors Maya’s psychological state, creating a sense of intimacy and immediacy.

Content and Analysis

Plot Overview

The novel revolves around Maya, a young, sensitive woman married to Gautama, a pragmatic and emotionally distant lawyer. The story begins with the death of Maya’s pet dog, Toto, an event that triggers a series of memories and reflections, ultimately leading to her psychological breakdown.

Maya’s relationship with Gautama is central to the novel. While Maya craves love, attention, and emotional connection, Gautama is detached and preoccupied with his work. This emotional incompatibility creates a growing sense of isolation and despair in Maya, who begins to feel trapped in her marriage.

As the narrative unfolds, Maya becomes increasingly obsessed with a prophecy made by an astrologer during her childhood, predicting that either she or her husband would die within four years of their marriage. This prophecy, combined with her loneliness and suppressed emotions, drives her toward madness. The novel culminates in a tragic and shocking climax, where Maya, unable to cope with her inner turmoil, kills Gautama and succumbs to her own psychological collapse.

Key Themes

1\. Alienation and Loneliness

Maya’s sense of alienation is a central theme of the novel. Despite being surrounded by people, she feels profoundly alone, unable to connect with her husband or find solace in her environment. Her loneliness is exacerbated by Gautama’s indifference and her own heightened sensitivity:

“I was alone, as I had been alone all my life.”

Maya’s isolation reflects the broader theme of existential loneliness, a recurring motif in Desai’s works.

2\. Marital Discord

The novel explores the emotional and psychological impact of an incompatible marriage. Maya and Gautama represent two opposing worldviews: Maya is emotional, imaginative, and deeply connected to her inner world, while Gautama is rational, detached, and focused on external realities. This fundamental disconnect creates a chasm between them, leading to frustration and despair:

“He did not understand, he did not care. He lived in a world of his own, apart from mine.”

Through their relationship, Desai critiques the patriarchal structures that prioritize duty and practicality over emotional fulfillment in marriage.

3\. Psychological Instability

Maya’s descent into madness is portrayed with remarkable psychological depth. Her mental instability is rooted in her childhood, marked by overprotection from her father and the astrologer’s ominous prophecy. As the narrative progresses, her fears and anxieties intensify, culminating in hallucinations and obsessive thoughts:

“The prophecy haunted me, like a shadow that would not leave.”

Desai’s portrayal of Maya’s psychological state is both empathetic and unsettling, capturing the fragility of the human mind.

4\. Death and Fatalism

The theme of death pervades the novel, symbolized by the astrologer’s prophecy and the recurring imagery of peacocks, which are associated with both beauty and tragedy. Maya’s obsession with death reflects her fear of mortality and her inability to reconcile with the impermanence of life:

“Death lay all about me, in the air, in the wind, in the trees.”

The novel’s title, Cry, the Peacock, alludes to the mournful cry of peacocks during the monsoon season, a metaphor for Maya’s own anguish and longing.

5\. Gender and Patriarchy

Maya’s struggles are deeply rooted in the patriarchal structures of Indian society, which confine women to roles of subservience and emotional dependence. Her father’s overindulgence and Gautama’s indifference both reflect the ways in which women’s needs and desires are often ignored or misunderstood. Maya’s eventual breakdown can be seen as a rebellion against these constraints, though it comes at a tragic cost.

Symbolism and Imagery

1\. The Peacock

The peacock is a central symbol in the novel, representing both beauty and sorrow. Its cry is a metaphor for Maya’s own anguish, while its association with death and rebirth reflects the novel’s themes of mortality and renewal.

2\. The Astrologer’s Prophecy

The prophecy serves as a symbol of fatalism and the inescapability of destiny. It haunts Maya throughout the novel, shaping her actions and perceptions.

3\. Nature and the Environment

Desai uses vivid descriptions of nature to mirror Maya’s emotional state. The oppressive heat, the monsoon rains, and the lush yet suffocating environment all reflect her inner turmoil:

“The heat pressed down on me, heavy and unrelenting, like the weight of my thoughts.”

4\. The Pet Dog, Toto

Toto’s death at the beginning of the novel symbolizes the fragility of life and foreshadows the tragic events to come. Maya’s grief over Toto’s death also highlights her sensitivity and emotional dependence.

Narrative Style

Desai employs a stream-of-consciousness technique to delve into Maya’s inner world. The narrative is fragmented and nonlinear, shifting between past and present, memory and reality. This style allows readers to experience Maya’s thoughts and emotions directly, creating a sense of intimacy and immediacy.

The lyrical and poetic quality of Desai’s prose enhances the novel’s emotional impact, while the use of symbolism and imagery adds depth and complexity to the narrative.

Historical and Personal Context

Cry, the Peacock was written during a period of significant social and cultural change in India, as the country grappled with the challenges of modernization and the legacy of colonialism. Desai’s focus on the inner lives of her characters reflects a shift in Indian English literature toward more introspective and psychological themes.

As a woman writer, Desai brought a unique perspective to Indian literature, exploring the emotional and psychological experiences of women in a patriarchal society. Her portrayal of

Maya’s struggles reflects her broader interest in the complexities of human relationships and the impact of societal expectations on individual identity.

Q1: Who is the author of the novel "Cry, The Peacock"?

B) Anita Desai

C) Arundhati Roy

D) Vikram Seth

Answer: B

Ex- The text clearly states that "Cry, The Peacock" is the first novel by Anita Desai.

Q24: What happens to Maya after Gautama's death?

A) She is arrested

B) She is institutionalized

C) She remarries

D) She commits suicide

Answer: D

Ex- The text mentions that later, Maya commits suicide by jumping from another balcony.

Q25: What is the overriding theme of the novel?

A) Revenge

B) Love

C) Ambition

D) Betrayal

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that the overriding theme of this sorrowful novel is love, including parental love, maternal love, and conjugal love.

Q26: What does the author explore regarding love in childhood?

A) Its irrelevance to adult life

B) Its supreme importance to the development of a balanced individual

C) Its potential for causing neurosis

D) Its transient nature

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that the author explores the necessity of love in childhood, suggesting its supreme importance to the development of a balanced individual.

Q27: What is absent in Maya's life after she grows up and gets married?

A) Paternal love

B) Sibling love

C) Maternal love

D) Self-love

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that maternal love is also absent when Maya grows up and gets married, as she remains childless.

Q28: What is hinted at regarding Maya's relationship with her father?

A) A Freudian father-fixation

B) A distant relationship

C) A purely platonic bond

D) A business partnership

Answer: A

Ex- The text mentions that there is more than a hint of a Freudian father-fixation by Maya due to the excessive love from her father.

Q29: What is the more destructive factor in Maya's life?

A) The lack of love from her husband

B) The death of her pet dog

C) The absence of her brother

D) The loss of her mother

Answer: A

Ex- The text states that the more destructive factor in Maya's life is the lack of love from her husband, Gautama.

Q30: What drives Maya to her murderous madness?

A) The loss of love in childhood

B) The departure of her beloved brother

C) The lack of love in marriage

D) All of the above

Answer: D

Ex- The text mentions that the loss of love in childhood, the departure of a beloved brother, the lack of love in marriage, and the absence of a child as an object of a mother's love are what drive Maya to her murderous madness.

Q1: What is Maya's state of mind at the beginning of the story?

A) Joyful

B) Neurotic

C) Apathetic

D) Calm

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that even in the beginning of the story, Maya is a neurotic young woman, haunted by loss and death.

Q2: What tragedy befalls Maya on the first page of the novel?

A) Her father dies

B) Her husband dies

C) Her brother dies

D) Her pet dog dies

Answer: D

Ex- The text mentions that on the first page of the novel, Maya's pet dog dies.

Q3: Who predicted that either Maya or her husband would die within four years of their marriage?

A) A fortune teller

B) An astrologer

C) A priest

D) A doctor

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Maya is terrified by the prediction of an astrologer that either she or her husband would die within four years of their marriage.

Q4: What does Maya want from her husband that he is incapable of showing?

A) Wealth

B) Status

C) Warmth and love

D) Intelligence

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Maya wants warmth and love from her husband, which she got from her father, but which her husband is incapable of showing.

Q5: Who does Maya believe loves her the most?

A) Her mother

B) Her brother

C) Her husband

D) Her father

Answer: D

Ex- The text quotes Maya lamenting to herself that '\[n\]o one, no one else, loves me as my father does.'

Q6: What kind of person is Maya described as?

A) Extremely sensitive and sensuous

B) Cold and calculating

C) Outgoing and sociable

D) Rational and logical

Answer: A

Ex- The text describes Maya as an extremely sensitive and sensuous person, who wants 'contact that goes deeper than the flesh.'
