## Main study material

Chapter 7

(Note: Narrator in this chapter means ‘Maya’)

Q1: What kind of storm is described in the passage?

A) Rain-storm

B) Thunder-storm

C) Dust-storm

D) Snow-storm

Answer: C

Ex- The passage vividly describes a powerful dust-storm sweeping through the area.

Q2: How does the narrator describe the colors of the sky during the storm?

A) Soothing shades of blue and white

B) Harsh colors like yellow, sulphur, ochre and purple

C) Soft pastel hues

D) Monotonous gray tones

Answer: B

Ex- The author uses striking descriptions like "harsh yellow to strident sulphur, strident sulphur to bitter ochre" to paint the sky in vivid, strong colors.

Q3: What instrument do the girls next door practice every evening?

A) Piano

B) Violin

C) Flute

D) Sitar

Answer: D

Ex- The narrator mentions hearing the "high, tinny sound of the nimble sitar" as the girls practice their scales.

Q4: Which animal runs past the narrator and Gautama on the stairs in a state of alarm?

A) A dog

B) A cat

C) A rat

D) A bird

Answer: B

Ex- The passage states "a long dark figure suddenly sped past" and the narrator saw it was her cat, with "shocked eyes flashing in the dark".

Q5: What is the name of the defense lawyer Gautama mentions who he suspects of prolonging a case unnecessarily?

A) Arjuna

B) Krishnan

C) Toto

D) Maya

Answer: B

Ex- Gautama complains about a case being prolonged, saying "It's that damned defence lawyer...Krishnan — he'll go far, he's so unscrupulous".

Q6: Which deity's statue is described in the drawing room?

A) Vishnu

B) Brahma

C) Shiva

D) Krishna

Answer: C

Ex- The passage describes "the bronze Shiva, dancing just a shade outside the ring of lamplight" in the drawing room.

Q7: What is the parapet wall on the roof described as being low enough for?

A) Jumping over

B) Sitting on comfortably

C) Leaning against

D) Hiding behind

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator mentions that the parapet wall on the roof was "low enough to sit on, quite comfortably, as the crows did".

Q8: What does the narrator compare the moon to when she sees it rising?

A) A waxen white rose

B) A silver coin

C) A glowing lantern

D) A shimmering pearl

Answer: A

Ex- Upon seeing the moon rise, the narrator describes it as appearing like "a great multifoliate rose, waxen white, virginal, chaste and absolute white".

Q9: What happens to Gautama at the end of the passage?

A) He goes to sleep

B) He begins dancing

C) He falls from the roof

D) He starts an argument

Answer: C

Ex- In the final lines, the narrator thrusts her arms at Gautama in fury and sees "him fall then, pass through an immensity of air, down to the very bottom."

Q10: How does the narrator describe Gautama's detachment and preoccupation?

A) As admirable focus

B) As an armor defending him

C) As an endearing quality

D) As a pitiable flaw

Answer: B

Ex- Gautama's detachment is described as "forbidding armour" that the narrator knew defended him even when he seemed vulnerable.

Q11: What memory is evoked for the narrator by the scent of the lime trees?

A) Her wedding day

B) The death of her pet dog

C) Leaving her childhood home

D) Meeting Gautama for the first time

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator says the fragrance of the lime trees brought "flooding across" the memory of her dead dog lying under them.

Q12: What was Gautama's childhood education restricted by?

A) His parents' wishes

B) His own lack of interest

C) Economic constraints

D) Political turmoil

Answer: C

Ex- Gautama mentions his education was restricted to "the brief confines of a small, airless, chalky class-room in a far from fashionable school" as "Economy demanded that".

Q13: How does the narrator describe the storm making her feel?

A) Terrified and anxious

B) Relieved and exultant

C) Nostalgic and pensive

D) Angry and resentful

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator expresses joyful relief at surviving the storm, feeling a "liberation" and describing it as a "carnival to enjoy".

Q14: What does the narrator recognize about her attachment to Gautama despite their differences?

A) It made her more independent

B) It taught her heart new things, including pain

C) It stifled her true desires

D) It was purely a relationship of convenience

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator reflects that Gautama "taught it \[her heart\] pain" and "enlarged it, so that many new experiences could come within its compass".

Q15: What type of case is Gautama dealing with that he discusses with the narrator?

A) A property dispute

B) A murder trial

C) A corporate lawsuit

D) The passage does not specify

Answer: D

Ex- While Gautama mentions a prolonged court case, no details are provided about the type or nature of the case.

Q16: What sensation does the narrator have while walking on the roof with Gautama?

A) A sense of dizziness

B) Feeling disproportionately exhausted

C) Shivering from the cold night air

D) Elation at the beauty of the stars

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator states that despite barely being conscious of her footsteps, she "felt disproportionately exhausted, and the backs of my knees began to tremble".

Q17: How is the light from the rising moon described?

A) As harsh and glaring

B) As faint and flickering

C) As holy in its purity

D) As ominous and unsettling

Answer: C

Ex- The moon is said to cast "a light that was holy in its purity, a soft, suffusing glow of its chastity".

Q18: What does the narrator compare Gautama to as he paces on the roof, preoccupied with his thoughts?

A) A caged animal

B) A sleepwalker

C) A lost child

D) A man on a mission

Answer: B

Ex- Gautama is described as pacing on "a somnambulist's figure in opium-white", comparing him to someone sleepwalking in a trance-like state.

Q19: What emotion does the narrator express feeling for Gautama despite their differences?

A) Admiration

B) Pity

C) Envy

D) Hatred

Answer: B

Ex- At various points the narrator expresses pity for Gautama, thinking "Poor Gautama, poor dear Gautama who was so intense and yet had never lived, and never would."

Q20: How does the narrator describe the garden at night?

A) As a soothing refuge

B) As an eerie jungle stirring with uneasy life

C) As a barren wasteland

D) As a cheerful space filled with night-blooming flowers

Answer: B

Ex- The gardens surrounding the bungalow at night are described as "turning inkier and inkier" with "the uneasy life of a jungle at night stirring through them".

Q21: What startles Gautama out of his preoccupied thoughts on the roof?

A) The narrator's scream

B) A loud clap of thunder

C) The narrator touching his arm

D) A shooting star in the sky

Answer: C

Ex- When the narrator finds Gautama's prolonged silence unbearable, she touches his arm and cries out, which "startled" him out of his thoughts.

Q22: How does the narrator describe the oppressive heat before the storm?

A) As dry and withering

B) As thick and oily, like a physical presence

C) As hazy and shimmering with mirages

D) As heavy with impending rain

Answer: B

Ex- The intense heat is described as "oozing into the room, pouring in like thick, warm oil, swelling and expanding till it became physical, a presence that pressed against one's body".

Q23: What does the narrator compare the dust-cloaked, sun-scorched garden to?

A) A desert wasteland

B) A pressed flower, fading to dust

C) A graveyard of withered plants

D) A sepia-toned photograph

Answer: B

Ex- The garden is described as lying "pinioned by the sun, like a great pressed flower, pressed so long ago that it was now quite hopeless, dry, fading fast to assume the colour and, finally, the nature of dust."

Q24: What sound does the narrator imagine the squeals of the bats to be like?

A) Shrill cries of distress

B) Bits of stars tinkling to earth

C) High-pitched laughter

D) Eerie whispers in the dark

Answer: B

Ex- The narrator fancifully describes the small squeals emitted by the bats as sounding as though they "might have been bits of stars tinkling to earth".

Q25: How does the narrator describe Gautama's skin after his bath?

A) Radiant and glowing

B) Wrinkled and weathered

C) Anaemic and stretched tightly over his bones

D) Smooth and youthful

Answer: C

Ex- Gautama's post-bath appearance is described as "His anaemic skin stretched on the downward, curving bones of his cage-like ribs".

Q26: What effect does the narrator say Gautama had on her heart?

A) He made it colder and more closed off

B) He made it more fickle and capricious

C) He made it more passionate and impulsive

D) He enlarged it to encompass more experiences

Answer: D

Ex- The narrator states "He enlarged it \[her heart\], so that many new experiences could come within its compass".

Q27: What image symbolizes Gautama's tenuous connection to the narrator's vivid world?

A) The flash of a shooting star

B) The dancing flame of his cigarette

C) The ticking of a distant clock

D) The cry of a bird in the night

Answer: B

Ex- Gautama's lit cigarette is described as seeming "the only thing about him that was alive, a lone antennae with which he kept in touch with the world, however shakily."

Q28: How does the narrator describe her love for Gautama despite her frustrations?

A) As a burden she resents

B) As a dream she longs to forget

C) As a duty she must fulfill

D) As something she expresses gratitude for

Answer: D

Ex- The narrator states "And so there was gratitude amidst all my other feelings when I turned and saw him come back," expressing thankfulness for the complex love between them.

Q29: What literary device is used in the description "a long dark figure suddenly sped past"?

A) Simile

B) Metaphor

C) Alliteration

D) Onomatopoeia

Answer: C

Ex- The phrase repeats the consonant sounds at the beginning of "long" "dark" and "figure" for a poetic effect, which is an example of alliteration.

Q30: What does the narrator compare her exhaustion on the roof to?

A) Being lost in a vast desert

B) Carrying a heavy physical burden

C) Drowning in a deep ocean

D) Climbing an endless flights of stairs

Answer: A

Ex- The narrator describes herself as walking on the roof until she can barely feel her footsteps, as though trudging through a seemingly endless expanse, similar to being lost in a desert.

Part III

### अभ्यास प्रश्न

1. What kind of storm is described in the passage?
   A. Rain-storm
   B. Thunder-storm
   C. Dust-storm
   D. Snow-storm
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The passage vividly describes a powerful dust-storm sweeping through the area.

2. How does the narrator describe the colors of the sky during the storm?
   A. Soothing shades of blue and white
   B. Harsh colors like yellow, sulphur, ochre and purple
   C. Soft pastel hues
   D. Monotonous gray tones
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The author uses striking descriptions like "harsh yellow to strident sulphur, strident sulphur to bitter ochre" to paint the sky in vivid, strong colors.

3. What instrument do the girls next door practice every evening?
   A. Piano
   B. Violin
   C. Flute
   D. Sitar
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator mentions hearing the "high, tinny sound of the nimble sitar" as the girls practice their scales.

4. Which animal runs past the narrator and Gautama on the stairs in a state of alarm?
   A. A dog
   B. A cat
   C. A rat
   D. A bird
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The passage states "a long dark figure suddenly sped past" and the narrator saw it was her cat, with "shocked eyes flashing in the dark".

5. What is the name of the defense lawyer Gautama mentions who he suspects of prolonging a case unnecessarily?
   A. Arjuna
   B. Krishnan
   C. Toto
   D. Maya
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama complains about a case being prolonged, saying "It's that damned defence lawyer...Krishnan — he'll go far, he's so unscrupulous".

6. Which deity's statue is described in the drawing room?
   A. Vishnu
   B. Brahma
   C. Shiva
   D. Krishna
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The passage describes "the bronze Shiva, dancing just a shade outside the ring of lamplight" in the drawing room.

7. What is the parapet wall on the roof described as being low enough for?
   A. Jumping over
   B. Sitting on comfortably
   C. Leaning against
   D. Hiding behind
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator mentions that the parapet wall on the roof was "low enough to sit on, quite comfortably, as the crows did".

8. What does the narrator compare the moon to when she sees it rising?
   A. A waxen white rose
   B. A silver coin
   C. A glowing lantern
   D. A shimmering pearl
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Upon seeing the moon rise, the narrator describes it as appearing like "a great multifoliate rose, waxen white, virginal, chaste and absolute white".

9. What happens to Gautama at the end of the passage?
   A. He goes to sleep
   B. He begins dancing
   C. He falls from the roof
   D. He starts an argument
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- In the final lines, the narrator thrusts her arms at Gautama in fury and sees "him fall then, pass through an immensity of air, down to the very bottom."

10. How does the narrator describe Gautama's detachment and preoccupation?
   A. As admirable focus
   B. As an armor defending him
   C. As an endearing quality
   D. As a pitiable flaw
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama's detachment is described as "forbidding armour" that the narrator knew defended him even when he seemed vulnerable.

11. What memory is evoked for the narrator by the scent of the lime trees?
   A. Her wedding day
   B. The death of her pet dog
   C. Leaving her childhood home
   D. Meeting Gautama for the first time
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator says the fragrance of the lime trees brought "flooding across" the memory of her dead dog lying under them.

12. What was Gautama's childhood education restricted by?
   A. His parents' wishes
   B. His own lack of interest
   C. Economic constraints
   D. Political turmoil
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama mentions his education was restricted to "the brief confines of a small, airless, chalky class-room in a far from fashionable school" as "Economy demanded that".

13. How does the narrator describe the storm making her feel?
   A. Terrified and anxious
   B. Relieved and exultant
   C. Nostalgic and pensive
   D. Angry and resentful
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator expresses joyful relief at surviving the storm, feeling a "liberation" and describing it as a "carnival to enjoy".

14. What does the narrator recognize about her attachment to Gautama despite their differences?
   A. It made her more independent
   B. It taught her heart new things, including pain
   C. It stifled her true desires
   D. It was purely a relationship of convenience
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator reflects that Gautama "taught it [her heart] pain" and "enlarged it, so that many new experiences could come within its compass".

15. What type of case is Gautama dealing with that he discusses with the narrator?
   A. A property dispute
   B. A murder trial
   C. A corporate lawsuit
   D. The passage does not specify
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- While Gautama mentions a prolonged court case, no details are provided about the type or nature of the case.

16. What sensation does the narrator have while walking on the roof with Gautama?
   A. A sense of dizziness
   B. Feeling disproportionately exhausted
   C. Shivering from the cold night air
   D. Elation at the beauty of the stars
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator states that despite barely being conscious of her footsteps, she "felt disproportionately exhausted, and the backs of my knees began to tremble".

17. How is the light from the rising moon described?
   A. As harsh and glaring
   B. As faint and flickering
   C. As holy in its purity
   D. As ominous and unsettling
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The moon is said to cast "a light that was holy in its purity, a soft, suffusing glow of its chastity".

18. What does the narrator compare Gautama to as he paces on the roof, preoccupied with his thoughts?
   A. A caged animal
   B. A sleepwalker
   C. A lost child
   D. A man on a mission
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama is described as pacing on "a somnambulist's figure in opium-white", comparing him to someone sleepwalking in a trance-like state.

19. What emotion does the narrator express feeling for Gautama despite their differences?
   A. Admiration
   B. Pity
   C. Envy
   D. Hatred
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- At various points the narrator expresses pity for Gautama, thinking "Poor Gautama, poor dear Gautama who was so intense and yet had never lived, and never would."

20. How does the narrator describe the garden at night?
   A. As a soothing refuge
   B. As an eerie jungle stirring with uneasy life
   C. As a barren wasteland
   D. As a cheerful space filled with night-blooming flowers
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The gardens surrounding the bungalow at night are described as "turning inkier and inkier" with "the uneasy life of a jungle at night stirring through them".

21. What startles Gautama out of his preoccupied thoughts on the roof?
   A. The narrator's scream
   B. A loud clap of thunder
   C. The narrator touching his arm
   D. A shooting star in the sky
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- When the narrator finds Gautama's prolonged silence unbearable, she touches his arm and cries out, which "startled" him out of his thoughts.

22. How does the narrator describe the oppressive heat before the storm?
   A. As dry and withering
   B. As thick and oily, like a physical presence
   C. As hazy and shimmering with mirages
   D. As heavy with impending rain
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The intense heat is described as "oozing into the room, pouring in like thick, warm oil, swelling and expanding till it became physical, a presence that pressed against one's body".

23. What does the narrator compare the dust-cloaked, sun-scorched garden to?
   A. A desert wasteland
   B. A pressed flower, fading to dust
   C. A graveyard of withered plants
   D. A sepia-toned photograph
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The garden is described as lying "pinioned by the sun, like a great pressed flower, pressed so long ago that it was now quite hopeless, dry, fading fast to assume the colour and, finally, the nature of dust."

24. What sound does the narrator imagine the squeals of the bats to be like?
   A. Shrill cries of distress
   B. Bits of stars tinkling to earth
   C. High-pitched laughter
   D. Eerie whispers in the dark
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator fancifully describes the small squeals emitted by the bats as sounding as though they "might have been bits of stars tinkling to earth".

25. How does the narrator describe Gautama's skin after his bath?
   A. Radiant and glowing
   B. Wrinkled and weathered
   C. Anaemic and stretched tightly over his bones
   D. Smooth and youthful
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama's post-bath appearance is described as "His anaemic skin stretched on the downward, curving bones of his cage-like ribs".

26. What effect does the narrator say Gautama had on her heart?
   A. He made it colder and more closed off
   B. He made it more fickle and capricious
   C. He made it more passionate and impulsive
   D. He enlarged it to encompass more experiences
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator states "He enlarged it [her heart], so that many new experiences could come within its compass".

27. What image symbolizes Gautama's tenuous connection to the narrator's vivid world?
   A. The flash of a shooting star
   B. The dancing flame of his cigarette
   C. The ticking of a distant clock
   D. The cry of a bird in the night
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Gautama's lit cigarette is described as seeming "the only thing about him that was alive, a lone antennae with which he kept in touch with the world, however shakily."

28. How does the narrator describe her love for Gautama despite her frustrations?
   A. As a burden she resents
   B. As a dream she longs to forget
   C. As a duty she must fulfill
   D. As something she expresses gratitude for
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator states "And so there was gratitude amidst all my other feelings when I turned and saw him come back," expressing thankfulness for the complex love between them.

29. What literary device is used in the description "a long dark figure suddenly sped past"?
   A. Simile
   B. Metaphor
   C. Alliteration
   D. Onomatopoeia
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The phrase repeats the consonant sounds at the beginning of "long" "dark" and "figure" for a poetic effect, which is an example of alliteration.

30. What does the narrator compare her exhaustion on the roof to?
   A. Being lost in a vast desert
   B. Carrying a heavy physical burden
   C. Drowning in a deep ocean
   D. Climbing an endless flights of stairs
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The narrator describes herself as walking on the roof until she can barely feel her footsteps, as though trudging through a seemingly endless expanse, similar to being lost in a desert. Part III Chapter 1

## Main study material

Chapter 1

Q1: What time of day is it at the beginning of the story?

A) 9:00 in the morning

B) 12:00 noon

C) 6:00 in the evening

D) 9:00 in the evening

Answer: D

Ex- The story states "It was nine o'clock in the evening" at the very beginning.

Q2: How long had the house in Lucknow been vacant?

A) 1 week

B) 2 weeks

C) 3 weeks

D) 1 month

Answer: D

Ex- The text mentions "After a month of vacancy, the house in Lucknow had been opened up".

Q3: Who informed the servants of the arrival of the women?

A) A phone call

B) A letter

C) A telegram

D) An email

Answer: C

Ex- It states the servants were "informed by one terse telegram of the arrival of the daughter of the house and the two women accompanying her".

Q4: What feature did the gardener suggest working on again?

A) The old fountain

B) The old gazebo

C) The old swing set

D) The old Mountain

Answer: D

Ex- The text says "the gardener had even suggested working the old Mountain again".

Q5: How many bedrooms were aired and prepared?

A) Two

B) Three

C) Four

D) Five

Answer: C

Ex- The story mentions "four bedrooms aired and prepared".

Q6: What was the atmosphere of the house compared to?

A) A hospital waiting room

B) An expensive nursing-home for convalescents

C) A funeral parlor

D) A summer holiday resort

Answer: B

Ex- The text states "Its atmosphere was more like that of an expensive nursing-home for convalescents."

Q7: What was the mother knitting?

A) A scarf

B) A sweater

C) Baby garments

D) A blanket

Answer: C

Ex- It mentions "She kept her fingers busy with baby garments that she was knitting".

Q8: Who was the mother knitting the garments for?

A) Her own baby

B) Her grandchild

C) A small orphan admitted to her creche

D) A neighbor's child

Answer: C

Ex- The mother was knitting "for a small orphan who had just been admitted into her creche as a special case".

Q9: What kind of dog was Nila trying to befriend?

A) A dachshund

B) A Pomeranian

C) A poodle

D) A Labrador

Answer: B

Ex- Nila "sat trying to make friends with a dim-witted Pomeranian".

Q10: Where was the telegram expected to arrive from?

A) England

B) America

C) Switzerland

D) Australia

Answer: C

Ex- When discussing the telegram, the mother says "But from Switzerland it will naturally take some time."

Q11: How many people originally lived in the large house?

A) Two

B) Three

C) Four

D) Five

Answer: A

Ex- Nila asks "How did they manage to live here- just the two of them?"

Q12: What kind of décor did the house have?

A) Minimalist modern

B) French country

C) Victorian hodge-podge

D) Traditional Indian

Answer: C

Ex- Nila complains about "the things that are crammed into it - a Victorian hodge-podge."

Q13: Who did they find a letter from in the desk?

A) Maya's father

B) Maya's husband

C) Maya's brother

D) Maya's lawyer

Answer: C

Ex- The mother mentions "There was a brother too. The one whose letter we found in the desk."

Q14: What was Maya doing upstairs?

A) Crying

B) Sleeping

C) Opening cupboards and pulling out drawers

D) Packing her belongings

Answer: C

Ex- They could hear Maya "delightedly opening cupboards, pulling out drawers".

Q15: How did Maya greet Nila and her mother when they arrived?

A) With tears

B) With anger

C) With a feline embrace

D) With indifference

Answer: C

Ex- Nila was "inflamed by the recollection of the girl greeting them with her feline embrace".

Q16: What did Maya insist on bringing Nila and her mother?

A) Coffee and cookies

B) Tea and sweets

C) Lemonade and cake

D) Juice and fruit

Answer: B

Ex- Maya insisted on "bringing them tea, sweets" even as she recounted her horrific story.

Q17: What did Maya do to Gautama according to her own account?

A) Slapped him

B) Pushed him hard so he fell

C) Locked him in a room

D) Poisoned his food

Answer: B

Ex- Maya recounts in a casual manner "So then I pushed him, hard, and he fell."

Q18: Where did Nila's mother say the fewer people that see and hear Maya's story the better?

A) At the police station

B) At the hospital

C) In general

D) At the funeral

Answer: C

Ex- When Nila worries about Maya's story getting out, her mother thoughtfully says "the less people that see her and hear her story, the better."

Q19: Where will the doctors have to be told Maya's story according to Nila?

A) At the hospital

B) At the courthouse

C) At the asylum

D) At the morgue

Answer: C

Ex- Nila says "the doctors will have to be told at the asylum when she is taken there."

Q20: What had the doctor already done for Maya?

A) Prescribed her medication

B) Diagnosed her illness

C) Scheduled her surgery

D) Arranged her funeral

Answer: B

Ex- The mother states matter-of-factly "The doctor has already diagnosed her illness."

Q21: Who did Maya say had to live between her and Gautama?

A) Gautama

B) Herself

C) Both of them

D) Neither of them

Answer: B

Ex- Maya insists with calm certainty that "It had to be one of us, you see, and it was so clear that it was I who was meant to live."

Q22: What did Nila and her mother have great faith in?

A) Religion

B) Family

C) Activity

D) Fate

Answer: C

Ex- When confronted with difficult memories, it's mentioned "They both had great faith in activity."

Q23: What was Nila's mother doing to keep busy?

A) Knitting

B) Reading

C) Cooking

D) Cleaning

Answer: A

Ex- To stay occupied, the mother's "hands flew about her wool and knitting needles in a blur".

Q24: What did Nila and her mother avoid as they worked on winding wool?

A) Eye contact

B) Speaking

C) Physical contact

D) All of the above

Answer: C

Ex- As they wound wool, Nila and her mother sat "scrupulously avoiding contact".

Q25: What did Nila's mother compare their controlled thoughts to?

A) A wild animal

B) A natural disaster

C) An explosion

D) A disease

Answer: C

Ex- Their controlled thoughts are described as "safe within her staff, would explode out of the bounds prescribed for them".

Q26: What shocking thing did Nila notice about her mother?

A) She was smiling

B) She was angry

C) She was on the verge of crying

D) She had fallen asleep

Answer: C

Ex- Nila is shocked to see "some new horrible, senile weakness in that mouth" indicating her mother was about to cry.

Q27: What broke the stillness of the dark garden and hush of the room?

A) A scream

B) Footsteps and laughter

C) A gunshot

D) Thunder

Answer: B

Ex- The stillness was broken by "footsteps, uncertain but swift, skipping and running down towards them" and "a child's laughter".

Q28: Where were the footsteps and laughter coming from?

A) The garden

B) The kitchen

C) The staircase

D) The library

Answer: C

Ex- The sounds seemed "very close, on the staircase perhaps."

Q29: What happened after the laughter stopped?

A) There was silence

B) There was a knock on the door

C) They heard a different voice calling out in dread

D) The power went out

Answer: C

Ex- After the laughter stopped, "they heard a different voice calling, shrilly and desperately, from some unimaginable realm of horror".

Q30: Who went to check on Maya after hearing her cries?

A) Nila

B) The mother

C) A servant

D) The doctor

Answer: B

Ex- The mother took charge, saying "Someone must go to her" and "hurried out of the room" to go to Maya.

Q1: Which award did Anita Desai's novel "Cry, the Peacock" win?

A) Booker Prize

B) Pulitzer Prize

C) Sahitya Academy Award

D) None of the above

Answer: D

Ex- "Cry, the Peacock" didn’t win any award, although she won Sahitya Academy Award for her novel Fire on the Mountain.

Q2: In which year was "Cry, the Peacock" published?

A) 1960

B) 1963

C) 1965

D) 1970

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that "Cry, the Peacock" was published in 1963.

Q3: What type of novel is "Cry, the Peacock"?

A) Historical novel

B) Science fiction novel

C) Psychological novel

D) Detective novel

Answer: C

Ex- The text describes "Cry, the Peacock" as a psychological novel.

Q4: Who is the heroine of the novel married to?

A) Gautama

B) Nirode

C) Albino

D) Ramsay

Answer: A

Ex- The text states that the heroine Maya is married to Gautama, a lawyer who is twice her age.

Q5: What does Desai focus on in the novel?

A) The loving relationship between Maya and Gautama

B) The detached relationship between Maya and Gautama

C) The friendship between Maya and Nila

D) The rivalry between Maya and Gautama's mother

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that Desai focuses on the detached relationship between Maya and Gautama.

Q6: What does the novel portray about Maya?

A) A most sensitive woman suffering from neurotic fears and marital disharmony

B) A happy and contented wife

C) A successful career woman

D) A rebellious teenager

Answer: A

Ex- The text states that the novel portrays Maya, a most sensitive woman suffering from neurotic fears and marital disharmony.

Q7: How does Maya fail to identify herself?

A) With her father's world

B) With her husband Gautama's world

C) With her own identity

D) With the modern world

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that Maya is a rebel woman who fails to identify herself with her husband Gautama's world.

Q8: What makes Maya feel insecure and powerless?

A) Her lack of education

B) Her total economic dependence on her husband

C) Her poor health

D) Her lack of friends

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Maya's total economic dependence on her husband makes her feel rather insecure and powerless.

Q9: What is Maya in search of?

A) A successful career

B) A new husband

C) A space in which she is at par with man

D) A way to escape from her life

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Maya is in search of a new vista for a woman's world, a space in which she is at par with man.

Q10: What does the novel suggest about modern life?

A) It is perfect and flawless

B) It is doomed to disorderliness and destruction unless it considers and nourishes the tender feelings of women

C) It is better than traditional life

D) It has no impact on women's lives

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that the novel clearly and evidently suggests that modern life is doomed to disorderliness and destruction unless it considers and nourishes the tender feelings of the women who form the nucleus in any society.

Q11: What do the writers of the post-Independence phase in India tend to do?

A) Focus on historical events

B) Move inward and try to get more psychologically intended

C) Write about science and technology

D) Ignore sociological effects on characters

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that in India, the writers of the post-Independence phase tend to move inward and try to get more and more psychologically intended.

Q12: Who have been able to see the Indian complexities from close quarters?

A) Male writers

B) Foreign writers

C) Women novelists

D) Children's authors

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that a number of women novelists have been able to see the Indian complexities from close quarters due to the peculiar situation of their existence.

Q13: What does fiction by women writers share?

A) A minor segment of contemporary writing in Indian English literature

B) A major segment of contemporary writing in Indian English literature

C) An insignificant part of Indian English literature

D) A declining trend in Indian English literature

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that fiction by women writers shares a major segment of the contemporary writing in Indian English literature.

Q14: What can one see through the eyes of women writers?

A) A different world

B) The same world as seen by male writers

C) A world devoid of human achievement

D) A world without dreams and desires

Answer: A

Ex- The text states that through the eyes of women writers, one can see a different world.

Q15: Who is Anita Desai considered to be among women novelists?

A) A minor voice

B) A controversial figure

C) A leading voice

D) A forgotten writer

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Anita Desai happens to be a leading voice among women novelists.

Q16: What does Desai tell in her novels?

A) Simple stories about ordinary people

B) Fascinating stories about individuals who have to traverse a ground too tricky and treacherous to handle smoothly

C) Boring stories about predictable characters

D) Unrealistic stories about perfect individuals

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Desai tells fascinating stories about individuals who have to traverse a ground too tricky and treacherous to handle smoothly.

Q17: What is Desai interested in analyzing and portraying?

A) Historical events

B) Scientific discoveries

C) Human relationships

D) Political ideologies

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Desai is interested in the analysis and portrayal of human relationships.

Q18: What is central to the main fictional concern in Desai's novels?

A) Historical accuracy

B) Political commentary

C) Human relationships

D) Scientific explanations

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that human relationships are not only of peripheral interest but also central to the main fictional concern in Desai's novels.

Q19: What does Desai's novel "Cry, the Peacock" explore?

A) The search of a woman to fulfil herself basically as a human being, independent of her traditional roles

B) The importance of women's traditional roles as daughter, wife, and mother

C) The superiority of men over women

D) The irrelevance of gender in modern society

Answer: A

Ex- The text mentions that "Cry, the Peacock" explores the search of a woman to fulfil herself basically as a human being, independent of her various traditional roles as daughter, wife, and mother.

Q20: Who determines Maya's fate in the novel?

A) Maya herself

B) The female characters in the novel

C) The male society - her father, husband, and Albino, the astrologer

D) The modern world

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that Maya's fate was determined by the male society - her father, husband, and Albino, the astrologer - who brought madness and death in her life.

Q21: What is Maya's only sin, according to the text?

A) She is disobedient and rebellious

B) She is lazy and unproductive

C) She is sensitive, imaginative, passionate, and sensuous

D) She is selfish and unkind

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Maya's only sin is that she is sensitive, imaginative, passionate, and sensuous.

Q22: Who does Maya represent in the novel?

A) The content and satisfied woman

B) The disturbed psyche of the modern Indian woman

C) The ideal wife and mother

D) The successful career woman

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Maya represents the disturbed psyche of the modern Indian woman.

Q23: What does the quest of the protagonist in "Cry, the Peacock" symbolize?

A) The quest of one woman only

B) The quest of all men in the society

C) The quest of all women in the society towards understanding and acceptance of their predicament

D) The quest for material wealth and success

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that the quest of the protagonist in the novel is not only one woman's quest but the quest of all women in the society towards some understanding and acceptance of their predicament.

Q24: Who are the other women characters in the novel who symbolically present positivity and strength?

A) Maya's mother and sister

B) Gautama's sister and aunt

C) Nila and Gautama's mother

D) Maya's friends and neighbors

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that apart from Maya, there are other women in the novel, such as Nila and Gautama's mother, who symbolically present positivity and strength.

Q25: What does the beginning of the novel tell about the relationship between Maya and Gautama?

A) Their loving and harmonious relationship

B) The husband-wife alienation theme

C) Their friendship and camaraderie

D) Their professional rivalry

Answer: B

Ex- The text mentions that the very beginning of the novel tells the husband-wife alienation theme by unfolding the relationship of Maya and Gautama.

Q26: What was the result of Maya's marriage to Gautama?

A) Maya found happiness and fulfilment

B) Maya became a successful lawyer like her husband

C) Maya became violent and killed her husband

D) Maya and Gautama had many children

Answer: C

Ex- The text states that the marriage was never fruitful, and as a result, at the end, Maya became violent and killed her husband.

Q27: What did Maya suffer from due to her introversion?

A) Physical illness

B) Financial problems

C) Utmost sexual dissatisfaction

D) Professional failure

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Maya failed to establish effective communication with her husband as she was an introvert and suffered from utmost sexual dissatisfaction.

Q28: What is Maya's life shadowed by?

A) The three-fold effect of love, happiness, and success

B) The three-fold effect of death, doom, and destiny

C) The three-fold effect of wealth, fame, and power

D) The three-fold effect of education, career, and marriage

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Maya's life is shadowed by the three-fold effect of death, doom, and destiny.

Q29: What does Maya's act of murder represent?

A) A random act of violence

B) A sign of her love for her husband

C) A revolt against the callous materialistic social order

D) A way to gain financial independence

Answer: C

Ex- The text mentions that Maya's act of murder is a revolt against the callous materialistic social order.

Q30: What distinguishes Anita Desai from other Indian writers?

A) Her focus on historical events

B) Her involvement with Indian women and their reactions

C) Her portrayal of perfect marriages

D) Her avoidance of psychological themes

Answer: B

Ex- The text states that Desai's involvement with Indian women and their reactions is different from others, which distinguishes her from other Indian writers.

Answer: (4) hyperbole

A long lyric poem, serious in subject matter, elevated in style, elaborate in its stanzaic structure is:

(1) an elegy

(2) a lyric

(3) an ode

(4) a dramatic monologue

Answer: (3) an ode

"Can honour's voice provoke the silent dust" is an example of:

(1) metonymy

(2) personification

(3) allegory

(4) metaphor

Answer: (2) personification

The Grail legend in T. S. Eliot's "The Waste Land" is taken from:

(1) Jessie L. Weston's "From Ritual to Romance"

(2) Dante's "The Divine Comedy"

(3) Chaucer's "Prologue to the Canterbury Tales"

(4) Ovid's "Metamorphosis"

Answer: (1) Jessie L. Weston's "From Ritual to Romance"

The last lines of the poem "The Waste Land" make use of the scriptural writing from:

(1) Buddha's "Fire Sermon"

(2) Edmund Spenser's "Prothalamion"

(3) "Brihadaranyaka Upanishad"

(4) James Frazer's "The Golden Bough"

Answer: (3) "Brihadaranyaka Upanishad"

The fourth section of the poem "The Waste Land" is titled:

(1) What the Thunder Said

(2) Death by Water

(3) A Game of Chess

(4) The Fire Sermon

Answer: (4) The Fire Sermon

"The Birthday Party" by Harold Pinter is a:

(1) Tragicomedy

(2) Comedy

(3) Comedy of Menace

(4) Farce

Answer: (3) Comedy of Menace

Stanley Webber in Pinter's "The Birthday Party" is:

(1) a heavyweight champion

(2) an erstwhile piano player

(3) a stock-broker

(4) a life-insurance agent

Answer: (2) an erstwhile piano player

Maya in Anita Desai's "Cry, The Peacock" is haunted by:

(1) memories of a loving mother

(2) childhood prophecy of a fatal disaster

(3) an affair from her past

(4) ghosts and devils

Answer: (2) childhood prophecy of a fatal disaster

What does Maya in "Cry, The Peacock" yearn for?

(1) money and fame

(2) social prestige

(3) love and respect

(4) long-lost lover

Answer: (3) love and respect

A stable natural language that develops from a pidgin is:

(1) Hinglish

(2) Creole

(3) Isogloss

(4) Code-mixing

Answer: (2) Creole

"Life ko face kijiye with himmat and faith in apane aap" is an example of:

(1) pidgin

(2) code-switching

(3) diglossia

(4) creole

Answer: (2) code-switching

"Code-Mixing" is:

(1) being able to use many languages fluently

(2) using words from one language only

(3) making use of reduced structures only

(4) using words from two languages together

Answer: (4) using words from two languages together

## 2015

Who replies to Milton's question "Doth God exact day-labour, light denied" in "On His Blindness"?

(1) Milton's inner voice

(2) God himself

(3) Patience

(4) Murmur

Answer: (3) Patience

How many impossible tasks does Donne enumerate in the first stanza of his poem "Goe, and catche a falling starre"?

(1) Seven

(2) Six

(3) Five

(4) Four

Answer: (2) Six

How far does Donne in his poem "Goe and catche a falling starre", advise the seeker of a true woman to travel?

(1) To the farthest poles of the earth.

(2) For 10,000 days and nights.

(3) Into the centre of the earth.

(4) Throughout the universe

Answer: (2) For 10,000 days and nights.

Where exactly did Wordsworth first espy the ten thousand daffodils mentioned in his poem "Daffodils"?

(1) high on the hills and vales.

(2) on the milky way at night.

(3) beside the lake, beneath the trees.

(4) beside the trees.

Answer: (3) beside the lake, beneath the trees.

When according to Wordsworth do the daffodils "flash upon his inward eye"?

(1) When he is in jocund company.

(2) When he is in a vacant and pensive mood.

(3) When he is on his wanderings.

(4) When he is asleep and dreaming.

Answer: (2) When he is in a vacant and pensive mood.

How does Shelley in his "Ode to West Wind" compare himself to the wind?

(1) as tumultuous, mighty and harmonious

(2) as tameless, swift and proud

(3) as impetuous and incanting

(4) as chained and bowed

Answer: (2) as tameless, swift and proud

In the last stanza of his "Ode to the West Wind" Shelley invokes the West Wind to make him his

(1) Ashes

(2) Prophecy

(3) Lyre

(4) Companion

Answer: (3) Lyre

Keats addresses the Grecian Urn as an "Attic Shape" in his "Ode on a Grecian Urn" because it was

(1) from Greece

(2) preserved in an attic

(3) placed in a Greek museum

(4) a receptacle for ashes

Answer: (1) from Greece

What, according to Keats, will the Urn remain to man, when 'old age shall this generation waste'?

(1) a forgotten historian

(2) a friend

(3) a silent spectator of his woes

(4) an art desolate

Answer: (2) a friend

What, according to the Duke in "My Last Duchess", called that "spot of joy" into the Duchess' cheek besides the presence of her husband?

(1) a gift of nine-hundred years old name.

(2) a courteous remark made by the painter

(3) a bough of cherries presented by an officious fool.

(4) the dropping of sunlight in the West

Answer: (2) a courteous remark made by the painter

The theme of Nissim Ezekiel's poem "Night of the Scorpion" is

(1) Selfless motherhood

(2) Helpless husbands

(3) Hopeless children

(4) Restless spectators

Answer: (1) Selfless motherhood

How did the villagers in "Night of the Scorpion" by Nissim Ezekiel react to the unfortunate incident?

(1) They stayed away in fear.

(2) They thronged to the temple to invoke the deity.

(3) They cursed and abused the mother for bringing upon them the wrath of god.

(4) They came in swarms to console the family.

Answer: (4) They came in swarms to console the family.

Why had the Scorpion entered the house in Ezekiel's "Night of the Scorpion"?

(1) He had a diabolic plan.

(2) He wanted to test the patience of the villagers.

(3) He was seeking protection from the steady rain.

(4) He had been conjured up by fate.

Answer: (3) He was seeking protection from the steady rain.

What was not a part of the attire of the eunuchs in Kamala Das' poem "Dance of the Eunuchs"?

(1) Wide skirts

(2) Heavy silver jewellery

(3) Jingling anklets

(4) Green tattoos on the cheeks

Though the dance of the eunuchs, in Kamala Das' poem by the same name, was loud and frenzied, their songs were

(1) enchanting and soothing

(2) spiritual and peaceful

(3) sad and melancholy

(4) of fairies and elves

Answer: (3) sad and melancholy

Despite the visual richness of the poem "Dance of the Eunuchs" Kamala Das creates an atmosphere of

(1) Gloom and depression

(2) Overpowering hilarity

(3) Joy and pleasantness

(4) Light gladness

Answer: (1) Gloom and depression

In Shakespeare's Macbeth who is Ross referring to when he uses the description "Bellona's Bridegroom"?

(1) Banquo

(2) MacDonald

(3) Macbeth

(4) Fleance

Answer: (3) Macbeth

"Was the hope drunk, wherein you dressed yourself"? What hope is Lady Macbeth talking about in Shakespeare's play Macbeth?

(1) to kill Macduff

(3) to get the "damned spot" of blood out

(4) to believe that Banquo's ghost would not plague Macbeth.

Which of the following is not a song from Shakespeare's As You Like It?

(1) Under the greenwood tree

(2) Blow, blow, thou winter wind

(4) Here we go round the Mulberry Bush.

Answer: (4) Here we go round the Mulberry Bush.

Who is it in As You Like It that tutors Orlando how to be a better lover?

(1) Phoebe

(2) Ganymede

(1) the great epic Gita.

(1) a beer-vending machine

(2) a sweet-making machine

(3) a story-writing machine

Milton's "On His Blindness" ends with an expression of the poet's:

Who in Milton's "On His Blindness" is believed to serve God best?

(2) One who presents a true account of his talent.

(3) One who doesn't allow talent to become useless.

Answer: (1) One who bears his burden best.

Of all the 'strange wonders', the strangest in Donne's poem 'Go Catch a Falling Star' would be:

(3) to learn to hear mermaids sing

(2) The hair-blackener

A poem in which a single speaker at a critical moment addresses another whose presence is implicit in what the speaker says is:

(1) a soliloquy

(2) an aside

(3) a dialogue

"The Waste Land" of T.S. Eliot is in the form of:

(1) fragmentary monologues

(2) an extended dialogue

(3) a discursive debate

(4) a literary discussion

Pick out the literary source to which Eliot owes the title of his poem 'The Waste Land':

(1) The Golden Bough

(2) From Ritual to Romance

(3) Metamorphoses

(4) Heart of Darkness

Answer: (2) From Ritual to Romance

Menace is orchestrated by Pinter in The Birthday Party by subtle devices. Which of the following is most Pinteresque in evoking menace?

(1) Meg and Petey's banal exchanges about what the newspaper reports

(2) Meg's evening dress

(3) McCann and Goldberg's destructive quizzing of Stanley

(4) Lulu's flirtations

Answer: (3) McCann and Goldberg's destructive quizzing of Stanley

The world in Anita Desai's 'Cry, the Peacock' centres on:

(1) the sylvan countryside

(2) the protagonist's obsession with the past

(3) the fullness and promise of the present

(4) the protagonist's hopes for the future

Answer: (2) the protagonist's obsession with the past

The major action of the novel 'Cry, the Peacock' takes place:

(1) in the garden

(2) in the city club

(3) inside the bungalow

(4) in Maya's mind

Answer: (4) in Maya's mind

A limited speech code useful only in a limited situation is called:

(1) Idiolect

(2) Creole

(3) Pidgin

(4) Dialect

Answer: (3) Pidgin

A mixed language that develops into a full language and acquires native users is:

(1) Creole

(2) Idiolect

(3) Lingua franca

(4) Diglossia

Answer: (1) Creole

Which of the following statements is correct?

(1) Switching from one code to another is a matter of individual choice.

(2) Code-switching is a strategy adopted in response to situational features.

(3) Code-switching is restricted to multilingual societies.

(4) Code-switching is not context governed.

Answer: (2) Code-switching is a strategy adopted in response to situational features.

When was Milton's "On His Blindness" first published?

(1) A burden which oppresses or restrains a person

(2) Garment or a piece of jewelry

(3) Mild Drink

(4) Treatment

Answer: (1) A burden which oppresses or restrains a person

Milton's poem "On His Blindness" is a/an:

(1) Elegy

(2) Sonnet

(3) Ballad

(4) Epic

(3) Michael Henchard and Lucetta

(4) Jopp and Lucetta

Answer: (3) Michael Henchard and Lucetta

"The Three Mariners" in the Novel, The Mayor of Casterbridge is:

(1) An assembly hall

(2) A market place

(3) An inn

(4) The Mayor's residence

Answer: (3) An inn

What does Jagan take with him to Chinna Dorai's grove?

(1) All the letters of Mali.

(2) All of his religious books.

(3) His Gandhian Charka and the "bank-book".

(4) Pictures of his wife.

Answer: (3) His Gandhian Charka and the "bank-book".

"Malgudi" is the name of a:

(1) Character

(2) Fictional town

(3) Sweet shop

(4) Book

Answer: (2) Fictional town

Identify the incorrect option.

(1) While reading the Gita Jagan's ears and mind are always in the kitchen.

(2) Jagan allows the leftovers to be restuffed in the new sweets.

(3) Jagan raises the price of the sweets.

(4) Jagan tells his cousin that money is an evil.

Answer: (2) Jagan allows the leftovers to be restuffed in the new sweets.

Identify the figure of speech in the lines below:

"Bide ten thousand days and nights, Till age snow white hairs on thee".

(1) Pun

(2) Hyperbole

(3) Simile

(4) Oxymoron

Answer: (2) Hyperbole

Identify the figure of speech:

"Loose clouds like earth's decaying leaves are shed".

(1) Simile

(2) Personification

(3) Metaphor

(4) Oxymoron

Answer: (1) Simile

The purification and purgation of emotions — particularly pity and fear — through art or any extreme change in emotion, that results in renewal and restoration is called:

(1) bathos

(2) conflict

(3) catharsis

(4) comic relief

Answer: (3) catharsis

An inoffensive expression used in place of a blunt one that is felt to be disagreeable or embarrassing, would be:

(1) Personification

(2) Transferred epithet

(3) Euphemism

(4) Pun

Answer: (3) Euphemism

A term to signify the distribution, at the end of a literary work, of earthly rewards and punishments in proportion to the virtue or vice of the characters is:

(1) climax

(2) tragic pleasure

(3) poetic justice

(4) poetic license

Answer: (3) poetic justice

A serious and dignified composition with an exalted subject matter and often in the form of an address is:

(1) Ode

(2) Lyric

(3) Epic

(4) Sonnet

Answer: (1) Ode

"The doves in immemorial elms And murmuring of innumerable bees" is an example of:

(1) Oxymoron

(2) Simile

(3) Onomatopoeia

(4) Pun

Answer: (3) Onomatopoeia

Mrs. Equitone in The Waste Land is a:

(1) fortune teller

(2) client of Madame Sosostris

(3) lady typist

(4) chess player

Answer: (2) client of Madame Sosostris

In which of the following scenes of The Waste Land do we have a departure from standard English?

(1) The typist scene

(2) The pub scene

(3) The hyacinth garden scene

(4) The chapel perilous scene

Answer: (2) The pub scene

Identify the Incorrect option:

(1) Goldberg and McCaan are embodiment of Stanley's existential anxiety.

(2) Goldberg and McCaan are society's 'pull' to claim back an 'opt-out' existence.

(3) Goldberg and McCaan can be called messengers of death and destruction.

(4) Goldberg and McCann are the artistic personification/side of Stanley.

Answer: (4) Goldberg and McCann are the artistic personification/side of Stanley.

What does Stanley get as his Birthday gift?

(1) a teddy bear

(2) a piano

(3) a toy drum

(4) a black car

Answer: (3) a toy drum

Pick out the central character in the novel, Cry the Peacock from the options below:

(1) Nargis

(2) Maya

(3) Gautami

(4) Daisy

Answer: (2) Maya

Cry the Peacock does not have theme of:

(1) alienation and isolation.

(2) marital disharmony and desire to be loved.

(3) inability of the inner self to communicate with the outer world.

(4) Class struggle and nationalism.

Answer: (4) Class struggle and nationalism.

A contact language, mixture of elements from different natural languages and the use of which is usually restricted to a certain group, is called a:

(1) Dialect

(2) Idiolect

(3) Pidgin

(4) Code

Answer: (3) Pidgin

When a Pidgin becomes a lingua franca, it is called a:

(1) Register

(2) Dialect

(3) Creole

(4) Englishs

Answer: (3) Creole

The shift from one language to the other consciously or unconsciously during speech is known as:

(1) Lingua Franca

(2) Code Switching

(3) Diglossia

(4) Register

Answer: (2) Code Switching

'Dil Mange More' is an example of:

(1) Code Neutralising

(2) Creole

(3) Code Mixing

(4) Coding

Answer: (3) Code Mixing

### अभ्यास प्रश्न

1. What time of day is it at the beginning of the story?
   A. 9:00 in the morning
   B. 12:00 noon
   C. 6:00 in the evening
   D. 9:00 in the evening
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The story states "It was nine o'clock in the evening" at the very beginning.

2. How long had the house in Lucknow been vacant?
   A. 1 week
   B. 2 weeks
   C. 3 weeks
   D. 1 month
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions "After a month of vacancy, the house in Lucknow had been opened up".

3. Who informed the servants of the arrival of the women?
   A. A phone call
   B. A letter
   C. A telegram
   D. An email
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- It states the servants were "informed by one terse telegram of the arrival of the daughter of the house and the two women accompanying her".

4. What feature did the gardener suggest working on again?
   A. The old fountain
   B. The old gazebo
   C. The old swing set
   D. The old Mountain
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text says "the gardener had even suggested working the old Mountain again".

5. How many bedrooms were aired and prepared?
   A. Two
   B. Three
   C. Four
   D. Five
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The story mentions "four bedrooms aired and prepared".

6. What was the atmosphere of the house compared to?
   A. A hospital waiting room
   B. An expensive nursing-home for convalescents
   C. A funeral parlor
   D. A summer holiday resort
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states "Its atmosphere was more like that of an expensive nursing-home for convalescents."

7. What was the mother knitting?
   A. A scarf
   B. A sweater
   C. Baby garments
   D. A blanket
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- It mentions "She kept her fingers busy with baby garments that she was knitting".

8. Who was the mother knitting the garments for?
   A. Her own baby
   B. Her grandchild
   C. A small orphan admitted to her creche
   D. A neighbor's child
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The mother was knitting "for a small orphan who had just been admitted into her creche as a special case".

9. What kind of dog was Nila trying to befriend?
   A. A dachshund
   B. A Pomeranian
   C. A poodle
   D. A Labrador
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila "sat trying to make friends with a dim-witted Pomeranian".

10. Where was the telegram expected to arrive from?
   A. England
   B. America
   C. Switzerland
   D. Australia
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- When discussing the telegram, the mother says "But from Switzerland it will naturally take some time."

11. How many people originally lived in the large house?
   A. Two
   B. Three
   C. Four
   D. Five
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila asks "How did they manage to live here- just the two of them?"

12. What kind of décor did the house have?
   A. Minimalist modern
   B. French country
   C. Victorian hodge-podge
   D. Traditional Indian
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila complains about "the things that are crammed into it - a Victorian hodge-podge."

13. Who did they find a letter from in the desk?
   A. Maya's father
   B. Maya's husband
   C. Maya's brother
   D. Maya's lawyer
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The mother mentions "There was a brother too. The one whose letter we found in the desk."

14. What was Maya doing upstairs?
   A. Crying
   B. Sleeping
   C. Opening cupboards and pulling out drawers
   D. Packing her belongings
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- They could hear Maya "delightedly opening cupboards, pulling out drawers".

15. How did Maya greet Nila and her mother when they arrived?
   A. With tears
   B. With anger
   C. With a feline embrace
   D. With indifference
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila was "inflamed by the recollection of the girl greeting them with her feline embrace".

16. What did Maya insist on bringing Nila and her mother?
   A. Coffee and cookies
   B. Tea and sweets
   C. Lemonade and cake
   D. Juice and fruit
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Maya insisted on "bringing them tea, sweets" even as she recounted her horrific story.

17. What did Maya do to Gautama according to her own account?
   A. Slapped him
   B. Pushed him hard so he fell
   C. Locked him in a room
   D. Poisoned his food
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Maya recounts in a casual manner "So then I pushed him, hard, and he fell."

18. Where did Nila's mother say the fewer people that see and hear Maya's story the better?
   A. At the police station
   B. At the hospital
   C. In general
   D. At the funeral
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- When Nila worries about Maya's story getting out, her mother thoughtfully says "the less people that see her and hear her story, the better."

19. Where will the doctors have to be told Maya's story according to Nila?
   A. At the hospital
   B. At the courthouse
   C. At the asylum
   D. At the morgue
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila says "the doctors will have to be told at the asylum when she is taken there."

20. What had the doctor already done for Maya?
   A. Prescribed her medication
   B. Diagnosed her illness
   C. Scheduled her surgery
   D. Arranged her funeral
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The mother states matter-of-factly "The doctor has already diagnosed her illness."

21. Who did Maya say had to live between her and Gautama?
   A. Gautama
   B. Herself
   C. Both of them
   D. Neither of them
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Maya insists with calm certainty that "It had to be one of us, you see, and it was so clear that it was I who was meant to live."

22. What did Nila and her mother have great faith in?
   A. Religion
   B. Family
   C. Activity
   D. Fate
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- When confronted with difficult memories, it's mentioned "They both had great faith in activity."

23. What was Nila's mother doing to keep busy?
   A. Knitting
   B. Reading
   C. Cooking
   D. Cleaning
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- To stay occupied, the mother's "hands flew about her wool and knitting needles in a blur".

24. What did Nila and her mother avoid as they worked on winding wool?
   A. Eye contact
   B. Speaking
   C. Physical contact
   D. All of the above
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- As they wound wool, Nila and her mother sat "scrupulously avoiding contact".

25. What did Nila's mother compare their controlled thoughts to?
   A. A wild animal
   B. A natural disaster
   C. An explosion
   D. A disease
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Their controlled thoughts are described as "safe within her staff, would explode out of the bounds prescribed for them".

26. What shocking thing did Nila notice about her mother?
   A. She was smiling
   B. She was angry
   C. She was on the verge of crying
   D. She had fallen asleep
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- Nila is shocked to see "some new horrible, senile weakness in that mouth" indicating her mother was about to cry.

27. What broke the stillness of the dark garden and hush of the room?
   A. A scream
   B. Footsteps and laughter
   C. A gunshot
   D. Thunder
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The stillness was broken by "footsteps, uncertain but swift, skipping and running down towards them" and "a child's laughter".

28. Where were the footsteps and laughter coming from?
   A. The garden
   B. The kitchen
   C. The staircase
   D. The library
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The sounds seemed "very close, on the staircase perhaps."

29. What happened after the laughter stopped?
   A. There was silence
   B. There was a knock on the door
   C. They heard a different voice calling out in dread
   D. The power went out
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- After the laughter stopped, "they heard a different voice calling, shrilly and desperately, from some unimaginable realm of horror".

30. Who went to check on Maya after hearing her cries?
   A. Nila
   B. The mother
   C. A servant
   D. The doctor
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The mother took charge, saying "Someone must go to her" and "hurried out of the room" to go to Maya.

31. Which award did Anita Desai's novel "Cry, the Peacock" win?
   A. Booker Prize
   B. Pulitzer Prize
   C. Sahitya Academy Award
   D. None of the above
   **उत्तर:** D
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- "Cry, the Peacock" didn’t win any award, although she won Sahitya Academy Award for her novel Fire on the Mountain.

32. In which year was "Cry, the Peacock" published?
   A. 1960
   B. 1963
   C. 1965
   D. 1970
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that "Cry, the Peacock" was published in 1963.

33. What type of novel is "Cry, the Peacock"?
   A. Historical novel
   B. Science fiction novel
   C. Psychological novel
   D. Detective novel
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text describes "Cry, the Peacock" as a psychological novel.

34. Who is the heroine of the novel married to?
   A. Gautama
   B. Nirode
   C. Albino
   D. Ramsay
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that the heroine Maya is married to Gautama, a lawyer who is twice her age.

35. What does Desai focus on in the novel?
   A. The loving relationship between Maya and Gautama
   B. The detached relationship between Maya and Gautama
   C. The friendship between Maya and Nila
   D. The rivalry between Maya and Gautama's mother
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Desai focuses on the detached relationship between Maya and Gautama.

36. What does the novel portray about Maya?
   A. A most sensitive woman suffering from neurotic fears and marital disharmony
   B. A happy and contented wife
   C. A successful career woman
   D. A rebellious teenager
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that the novel portrays Maya, a most sensitive woman suffering from neurotic fears and marital disharmony.

37. How does Maya fail to identify herself?
   A. With her father's world
   B. With her husband Gautama's world
   C. With her own identity
   D. With the modern world
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Maya is a rebel woman who fails to identify herself with her husband Gautama's world.

38. What makes Maya feel insecure and powerless?
   A. Her lack of education
   B. Her total economic dependence on her husband
   C. Her poor health
   D. Her lack of friends
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Maya's total economic dependence on her husband makes her feel rather insecure and powerless.

39. What is Maya in search of?
   A. A successful career
   B. A new husband
   C. A space in which she is at par with man
   D. A way to escape from her life
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Maya is in search of a new vista for a woman's world, a space in which she is at par with man.

40. What does the novel suggest about modern life?
   A. It is perfect and flawless
   B. It is doomed to disorderliness and destruction unless it considers and nourishes the tender feelings of women
   C. It is better than traditional life
   D. It has no impact on women's lives
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that the novel clearly and evidently suggests that modern life is doomed to disorderliness and destruction unless it considers and nourishes the tender feelings of the women who form the nucleus in any society.

41. What do the writers of the post-Independence phase in India tend to do?
   A. Focus on historical events
   B. Move inward and try to get more psychologically intended
   C. Write about science and technology
   D. Ignore sociological effects on characters
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that in India, the writers of the post-Independence phase tend to move inward and try to get more and more psychologically intended.

42. Who have been able to see the Indian complexities from close quarters?
   A. Male writers
   B. Foreign writers
   C. Women novelists
   D. Children's authors
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that a number of women novelists have been able to see the Indian complexities from close quarters due to the peculiar situation of their existence.

43. What does fiction by women writers share?
   A. A minor segment of contemporary writing in Indian English literature
   B. A major segment of contemporary writing in Indian English literature
   C. An insignificant part of Indian English literature
   D. A declining trend in Indian English literature
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that fiction by women writers shares a major segment of the contemporary writing in Indian English literature.

44. What can one see through the eyes of women writers?
   A. A different world
   B. The same world as seen by male writers
   C. A world devoid of human achievement
   D. A world without dreams and desires
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that through the eyes of women writers, one can see a different world.

45. Who is Anita Desai considered to be among women novelists?
   A. A minor voice
   B. A controversial figure
   C. A leading voice
   D. A forgotten writer
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Anita Desai happens to be a leading voice among women novelists.

46. What does Desai tell in her novels?
   A. Simple stories about ordinary people
   B. Fascinating stories about individuals who have to traverse a ground too tricky and treacherous to handle smoothly
   C. Boring stories about predictable characters
   D. Unrealistic stories about perfect individuals
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Desai tells fascinating stories about individuals who have to traverse a ground too tricky and treacherous to handle smoothly.

47. What is Desai interested in analyzing and portraying?
   A. Historical events
   B. Scientific discoveries
   C. Human relationships
   D. Political ideologies
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Desai is interested in the analysis and portrayal of human relationships.

48. What is central to the main fictional concern in Desai's novels?
   A. Historical accuracy
   B. Political commentary
   C. Human relationships
   D. Scientific explanations
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that human relationships are not only of peripheral interest but also central to the main fictional concern in Desai's novels.

49. What does Desai's novel "Cry, the Peacock" explore?
   A. The search of a woman to fulfil herself basically as a human being, independent of her traditional roles
   B. The importance of women's traditional roles as daughter, wife, and mother
   C. The superiority of men over women
   D. The irrelevance of gender in modern society
   **उत्तर:** A
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that "Cry, the Peacock" explores the search of a woman to fulfil herself basically as a human being, independent of her various traditional roles as daughter, wife, and mother.

50. Who determines Maya's fate in the novel?
   A. Maya herself
   B. The female characters in the novel
   C. The male society - her father, husband, and Albino, the astrologer
   D. The modern world
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Maya's fate was determined by the male society - her father, husband, and Albino, the astrologer - who brought madness and death in her life.

51. What is Maya's only sin, according to the text?
   A. She is disobedient and rebellious
   B. She is lazy and unproductive
   C. She is sensitive, imaginative, passionate, and sensuous
   D. She is selfish and unkind
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Maya's only sin is that she is sensitive, imaginative, passionate, and sensuous.

52. Who does Maya represent in the novel?
   A. The content and satisfied woman
   B. The disturbed psyche of the modern Indian woman
   C. The ideal wife and mother
   D. The successful career woman
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Maya represents the disturbed psyche of the modern Indian woman.

53. What does the quest of the protagonist in "Cry, the Peacock" symbolize?
   A. The quest of one woman only
   B. The quest of all men in the society
   C. The quest of all women in the society towards understanding and acceptance of their predicament
   D. The quest for material wealth and success
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that the quest of the protagonist in the novel is not only one woman's quest but the quest of all women in the society towards some understanding and acceptance of their predicament.

54. Who are the other women characters in the novel who symbolically present positivity and strength?
   A. Maya's mother and sister
   B. Gautama's sister and aunt
   C. Nila and Gautama's mother
   D. Maya's friends and neighbors
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that apart from Maya, there are other women in the novel, such as Nila and Gautama's mother, who symbolically present positivity and strength.

55. What does the beginning of the novel tell about the relationship between Maya and Gautama?
   A. Their loving and harmonious relationship
   B. The husband-wife alienation theme
   C. Their friendship and camaraderie
   D. Their professional rivalry
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that the very beginning of the novel tells the husband-wife alienation theme by unfolding the relationship of Maya and Gautama.

56. What was the result of Maya's marriage to Gautama?
   A. Maya found happiness and fulfilment
   B. Maya became a successful lawyer like her husband
   C. Maya became violent and killed her husband
   D. Maya and Gautama had many children
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that the marriage was never fruitful, and as a result, at the end, Maya became violent and killed her husband.

57. What did Maya suffer from due to her introversion?
   A. Physical illness
   B. Financial problems
   C. Utmost sexual dissatisfaction
   D. Professional failure
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Maya failed to establish effective communication with her husband as she was an introvert and suffered from utmost sexual dissatisfaction.

58. What is Maya's life shadowed by?
   A. The three-fold effect of love, happiness, and success
   B. The three-fold effect of death, doom, and destiny
   C. The three-fold effect of wealth, fame, and power
   D. The three-fold effect of education, career, and marriage
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Maya's life is shadowed by the three-fold effect of death, doom, and destiny.

59. What does Maya's act of murder represent?
   A. A random act of violence
   B. A sign of her love for her husband
   C. A revolt against the callous materialistic social order
   D. A way to gain financial independence
   **उत्तर:** C
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text mentions that Maya's act of murder is a revolt against the callous materialistic social order.

60. What distinguishes Anita Desai from other Indian writers?
   A. Her focus on historical events
   B. Her involvement with Indian women and their reactions
   C. Her portrayal of perfect marriages
   D. Her avoidance of psychological themes
   **उत्तर:** B
   **व्याख्या:** Ex- The text states that Desai's involvement with Indian women and their reactions is different from others, which distinguishes her from other Indian writers.
